Braided
L

Abstract
We define a new homotopy algebraic structure, that we call a braided L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra, and use it to systematically construct a new class of noncommutative field theories, that we call braided field theories. Braided field theories have gauge symmetries which realize a braided Lie algebra, whose Noether identities are inhomogeneous extensions of the classical identities, and which do not act on the solutions of the field equations. We use Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization techniques to generate new noncommutative deformations of classical field theories with braided gauge symmetries, which we compare to the more conventional theories with star-gauge symmetries. We apply our formalism to introduce a braided version of general relativity without matter fields in the Einstein–Cartan–Palatini formalism. In the limit of vanishing deformation parameter, the braided theory of noncommutative gravity reduces to classical gravity without any extensions.



EMPG–21–04

1
Introduction

In this paper we will present a new perspective on symmetries of noncommutative field theories, and develop first principles which enable systematic constructions of new examples of field theories with noncommutative gauge symmetries. To achieve this we develop a new notion of homotopy algebra that is suited to organise the symmetries and dynamical content of (generalized) noncommutative gauge theories; we call this new mathematical object a ‘braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra’. Although our formalism applies broadly to any perturbative classical field theory with symmetries, in this paper we focus exclusively on examples of diffeomorphism-invariant field theories for simplicity, in order to illustrate clearly and conceptually how our novel constructions work. In particular, we derive new noncommutative extensions of the Einstein–Cartan–Palatini formulation of gravity in three and four spacetime dimensions.

In this section we begin with a discussion of some background and context, before turning to a more detailed description of our results and the contents of this paper.

Classical field theories and
\bm{L_{\infty}}
-algebras

A
classical
field theory on a manifold can for a large part be understood as a dynamical system, that is, a system of partial differential equations which encodes the dynamics of fields. The field theory may have symmetries, particularly local symmetries, and we refer to these broadly as ‘generalized gauge symmetries’ to accomodate more general systems whose fields and symmetries are not necessarily associated with connections on a principal bundle. In the setting of dynamical systems, by ‘symmetry’ we mean that the field equations transform covariantly under the symmetry transformations; the gauge symmetry then acts on the space of classical solutions. The main object of interest is therefore the space of classical physical states, defined to be the moduli space of solutions to the field equations modulo the local symmetries. The field equations may or not follow from a variational principle. For a Lagrangian field theory, the data may be accompanied by a gauge-invariant action functional, however for many (but not all) considerations the action functional is supplementary data which is not needed to analyse the space of classical physical states.

This perspective is naturally and systematically organised by strong homotopy Lie algebras
[
41
]
, or
L_{\infty}
-algebras, which are generalizations of differential graded Lie algebras with infinitely-many graded antisymmetric brackets satisfying higher homotopy versions of the Jacobi identity. The data of any perturbative
1
1

1

In the case of a non-polynomial theory or a non-linear space of fields
\mathcal{M}
, this holds at the level of the pertubative expansion of the theory on the tangent space
T_{\phi}\mathcal{M}
at a fixed solution
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
.
classical field theory gives rise to an
L_{\infty}
-algebra. This may be seen in several ways. From a practical standpoint, the
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying a field theory may be constructed directly through an explicit “bootstrapping” algorithm
[
36
]
. They can also be obtained through the duality between
L_{\infty}
-algebras and differential graded commutative algebras, with the latter in this case being in the guise of the BV–BRST formalism
[
50
,
37
]
. These constructions can also be understood more geometrically in the setting of derived geometry, where the
L_{\infty}
-algebras which appear are the (derived) tangent complexes on the space of fields describing the deformation theory around critical points of an action functional
[
27
]
. For a Lagrangian field theory, the extra data of an action functional is captured by imposing an additional cyclic structure on the underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Noncommutative gauge theories and gravity

The dynamical systems perspective becomes particularly fruitful in situations where one is interested in a noncommutative deformation of a given field theory,
2
2

2

For us, this will be a field theory where the underlying spacetime manifold
M
is replaced by a noncommutative deformation of the algebra of functions on
M
, along with the relevant differential geometrical objects as modules over this algebra. Being a deformation of a commutative theory, the noncommutative structures and dynamics must reduce to the classical spacetime and theory in the commutative limit.
and in particular of gravity. For example, in the metric formulation of general relativity, it is often problematic to write down a variational principle which deforms the standard Einstein–Hilbert action functional, due to ambiguities in defining noncommutative deformations of volume forms and scalar curvatures, as well as reality properties and the inherent non-polynomial nature of the action (see e.g.
[
49
]
for a discussion of this). Yet consistent deformations of the field equations are straightforward (see e.g.
[
15
,
11
]
). It is then natural to seek
L_{\infty}
-algebras which organise symmetries and dynamics of noncommutative field theories.

The noncommutative extension of the bootstrap approach has been employed by
[
21
,
39
]
as a means for constructing new examples of noncommutative gauge theories. In this approach the
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework encodes not only the symmetries and dynamics of the field theory, but also the general failure of the deformed exterior algebra from forming a differential graded algebra and, in the case of nonassociative gauge symmetries, the failure of the Jacobi identities. This generally produces
L_{\infty}
-algebras with an infinite number of non-zero brackets, which has several limitations: explicit expressions for all brackets are difficult to obtain, the bootstrapping of brackets is particularly difficult and involved for nonassociative gauge symmetries, and so far this approach has only met with some success in the semi-classical limit of a noncommutative gauge theory. The approach based on symplectic embeddings
[
40
]
drastically improves the situation in that it allows for explicit and systematic constructions of all brackets of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra, but so far this approach is also restricted to the semi-classical limit and moreover limited to purely kinematic considerations.

In this paper we discuss a new alternative approach, which arose from our attempts to understand a noncommutative version of the Einstein–Cartan–Palatini theory of gravity in this language; the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism for the classical theory was developed in detail by
[
30
]
. In the standard noncommutative extension of this theory
[
4
]
, the (extended) local Lorentz symmetry is implemented by the usual star-gauge transformations, and in principle one may construct correspondingly an
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure as in the bootstrapping approach to noncommutative gauge theories. However, diffeomorphism invariance in this theory is implemented by the usual ‘twisted’ actions
[
15
]
(see
[
52
]
for a review), and the dichotomy between the actions of the two types of symmetries leads to severe difficulties in the implementation of an underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra as an organising principle for noncommutative gravity: The twisted diffeomorphism symmetry does not appear to fit naturally into the standard
L_{\infty}
-algebra picture. The situation is reminescent of what happens in double field theory
[
36
, Section 5.1]
, which requires extending the diffeomorphism symmetries by “higher” symmetries, implementing reducible gauge symmetries of the theory. In the case of noncommutative gravity this quickly becomes very complicated, and it is not clear how to manage the higher brackets of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra. In other words, the standard
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism does not seem to be an appropriate or useful tool for organising the dynamics of noncommutative gravity. Our proposal then is to deform the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure itself in such a way that it becomes compatible with the twisted diffeomorphisms. This leads to a new type of homotopy algebra and a new type of gauge symmetry for noncommutative field theories in general.

Braided
\bm{L_{\infty}}
-algebras and braided field theories

Following the classical treatment, we employ the machinery of Hopf algebras and Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization
3
3

3

We use the phrase “deformation quantization” colloquially to mean formal noncommutative deformation. Indeed, our theories will be classical in the physical sense, and no quantum physics
will take place in this manuscript.

[
43
]
to produce
braided
noncommutative deformations of field theories.
4
4

4

In this paper we use the term ‘braided’ as a synonym for ‘twisted’, as the former is more natural in the context in which our field theories are defined.
By twisting the enveloping Hopf algebra of vector fields on a manifold
M
to a non-cocommutative Hopf algebra and simultaneously deforming its category of modules, we deform the
L_{\infty}
-algebras into
braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras
, a structure that was sketched in
[
31
]
. In essence, one invokes the definition of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in terms of morphisms in a symmetric monoidal category with a non-trivial braiding. In practice, the axioms of a classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra are supplemented with a non-trivial isomorphism
{\mathcal{R}}:V\otimes V^{\prime}\to V\otimes V^{\prime}
whenever a transposition isomorphism between two modules
V
and
V^{\prime}
occurs. For example, antisymmetry is now braided:
\ell_{2}(v\otimes v^{\prime})=-\ell_{2}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(v^{\prime}\otimes v)
, and similarly for all higher homotopy Jacobi relations. Braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras may be regarded as a higher homotopy coherent extension of the notion of a braided Lie algebra, which was introduced by Woronowicz under the name ‘quantum Lie algebra’
[
56
]
, and later formulated in a more general categorical setting by Majid
[
42
]
.

When applied to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying a classical dynamical system, this procedure deforms the collection of kinematical and dynamical data simultaneously and consistently, producing a genuinely new type of noncommutative field theory, that we call a
braided field theory
. These are field theories with braided gauge symmetries which close as braided Lie algebras. The spaces of fields, or more precisely their tangent spaces, now form braided representations of these braided Lie algebras. Various aspects of the kinematical part of braided gauge symmetries have appeared before in e.g.
[
23
,
49
]
, but as far as we are aware a complete and general treatment of noncommutative dynamics with a variational principle has not been addressed before. From our perspective, the braided homotopy relations guarantee the braided representation properties, braided covariance of the field equations, and braided gauge invariance of the action functional. These symmetries differ from the star-gauge symmetries that are usually employed in noncommutative gauge theories, which close as classical Lie algebras. As a consequence, our braided deformations do not require the introduction of any new degrees of freedom or larger enveloping algebras to achieve gauge closure. We compare our braided gauge symmetries with the more conventional noncommutative gauge symmeties based on star-gauge transformations in Section
5.3
.

We point out and resolve a conceptual issue concerning braided gauge symmetries: they leave the field equations covariant and action functional invariant, but they do not act on the space of solutions of the field equations, contrary to the classical case. This is a consequence of the braided Leibniz rule of braided Lie algebra representation theory. However, the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework allows for the derivation of a braided version of the Noether identities, i.e. a set of differential identities among the field equations and the dynamical fields, which imply that not all degrees of freedom are independent. That is, some of them are gauge redundant, justifying the terminology ‘braided gauge symmetry’. The braided Noether identities are non-trivial deformations of their classical counterparts.

Braided noncommutative gravity

In this paper we apply our formalism of braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras to produce new braided noncommutative theories of gravity in three and four spacetime dimensions. We consider only the case of parallelizable spacetime manifolds
M
, for which the twist deformations are straightforward to implement and exhibit the salient features of our constructions more clearly; see
[
30
]
for the general treatment in the classical case. In three dimensions, the noncommutative field equations and action functional are naive deformations of the classical ones (with braided commutators), however in four dimensions we uncover non-trivial deformations. Both theories are crucially different from previous approaches for several reasons.

Firstly, in our braided approach the two sets of infinitesimal gauge symmetries, diffeomorphisms and local coframe rotations, each deform consistently with one another and together combine as a semi-direct product of braided Lie algebras, contrary to earlier approaches where diffeomorphisms are deformed as a braided Lie algebra but rotations as a classical Lie algebra (see e.g.
[
4
]
). Thus no new fields or extended enveloping algebras of symmetries need be introduced, contrary to these other approaches. Secondly, our approach formalises the treatment of braided gauge transformations and extends earlier treatments of twisted diffeomorphism symmetry of gravity to
all
gauge transformations, pointing out and clarifying several novel aspects of the space of solutions to the field equations compared to the unbraided case. Finally, one can in principle study solutions of the braided field equations, and compare our first order noncommutative theory of gravity in four dimensions with the existing metric formulation for noncommutative gravity (see e.g.
[
49
]
). We expect that these two theories are drastically different, but our approach enjoys an explicit action functional and the coupling to matter fields (such as fermions) is simply a straightforward calculation within the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework. We stress that the classical solution space of earlier treatments of the metric formulation of noncommutative gravity with twisted diffeomorphism symmetry also suffers from the same interpretational problem mentioned above, while our framework provides an explanation of the meaning of these symmetries in this sense. We compare our braided theories of gravity with other noncommutative theories of gravity in Section
6.1
.

Summary and outline of the paper

We begin in the next two sections by giving some background to the main developments of this paper. In Section
2
we review the basics of
L_{\infty}
-algebras, together with their cyclic versions, and how they are used to organise the symmetries and dynamics of classical field theories. We illustrate the formalism on the two main classes of examples of this paper: Chern–Simons gauge theory and the Einstein–Cartan–Palatini theory of gravity. In Section
3
we review the main ideas of Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization, using the enveloping Hopf algebra of vector fields on a manifold
M
, and look at the two particular examples of Hopf modules which form the backbone of this paper: the exterior algebra of differential forms on
M
and the algebras of vector fields on
M
themselves; these two modules also furnish our first examples of braided Lie algebras. At times we appeal to the categorical picture which interprets twist deformation quantization as a functorial equivalence of symmetric monoidal categories. This language is not strictly necessary to understand the main ideas or computations of this paper, but it does provide a convenient conceptual framework and at times simplifies what would otherwise be unwieldy technical computations.

In Section
4
, we come to the main mathematical definitions and constructions of this paper: we develop in detail the notion of a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra. It is here that the categorical language is particularly powerful, as it enables a straightforward formulation of the braided homotopy relations, which would be otherwise cumbersome and difficult to write out in general. Our definition makes sense in any symmetric monoidal category, and parallels the notion of a braided Lie algebra as a Lie algebra in such a category. When a classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra sits in the category of modules over the enveloping Hopf algebra of vector fields on
M
, we describe in detail how its Drinfel’d twist deformation defines a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra (see Proposition
4.8
). This result is very useful in applications, as once we are given a classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra, we know immediately that its twist quantization is a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra, without the need of having to go through lengthy and technically involved checks of the braided homotopy relations. We also consider twist quantization of cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras via Drinfel’d twists which are compatible with the cyclic structure in a suitable sense, and show that they produce strictly cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras (see Proposition
4.18
), a result which is important for applications to noncommutative field theories.

In Section
5
we turn to the main physical applications of the present paper. We formulate the notion of a braided field theory, taking the perspective that it defines a formal dynamical system. We give a general account of braided field theories starting from a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra as input. After a detailed general discussion of braided gauge symmetries, we proceed to explain how to build the kinematics and dynamics of a noncommutative field theory in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism, paralleling the constructions of the classical case. In general, the proofs of various properties such as gauge closure, braided covariance of the field equations, braided Noether identities and braided gauge invariance of an action functional are much more complicated compared to the classical case, as the tradeoff of strict graded antisymmetry to its braided version no longer allows simplification of the necessary homotopy relations. In particular, we find that braided covariance is realised non-trivially and results in the peculiar features of the space of classical physical states discussed above. The Noether identities also assume a very different form and contain inhomogeneous field-dependent terms compared to their classical versions.

Our construction gives a straightforward algorithm for producing braided field theories as noncommutative deformations of classical field theories:

1.

Given a classical field theory on a manifold
M
, work out its corresponding
L_{\infty}
-algebra, either by bootstrapping the initial brackets of its gauge transformations, field equations and Noether identities, or by using its dual BV–BRST formulation.

2.

Given an enveloping Hopf algebra of vector fields on
M
which generate symmetries of the classical field theory, and over which the
L_{\infty}
-algebra is a module, choose a corresponding Drinfel’d twist element and quantize the
L_{\infty}
-algebra to a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

3.

Run this braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra through the formulas of Section
5
to produce a noncommutative field theory with braided gauge symmetries.

We apply this algorithm to the example of Chern–Simons gauge theory in Section
5.9
. While the resulting braided field theory formally looks like a simple deformation of its classical version, it is quite different from the usual discussions of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory: in particular, it possesses braided gauge symmetries and its Noether identity is a deformation of the usual Bianchi identity. In general, the field equations and action functional of the resulting braided field theory will not simply be the “naive” deformations of those of the classical field theory, to which they nevertheless reduce in the classical limit: our algorithm constructs genuinely new examples of noncommutative gauge theories. We illustrate this in Section
6
by applying our formalism to construct new braided theories of noncommutative gravity in three dimensions, whose field equations are given by (
6.2
) and (
6.2
), and in four dimensions, whose field equations are given by (
6.3
) and (
6.3
).

Outlook

This paper arose as part of an ongoing programme to formulate a nonassociative theory of gravity that is inspired by scenarios in closed non-geometric string theory, see e.g.
[
11
,
18
,
20
]
. We argued in
[
30
]
that the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism should provide the natural receptacle to capture the failure of closure and covariance of field equations under nonassociative gauge transformations. To accomodate these instances, one may in principle deform the classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra using cochain twists of the enveloping algebra of vector fields, rather than cocycle twists, which results in a quasi-Hopf algebra. The end result will be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in a symmetric monoidal category with non-trivial braiding and associator isomorphisms. Using the corresponding deformed homotopy identites, nonassociative gauge transformations ought to have a straightforward definition, and the corresponding field equations and action functionals may be in reach within this formalism. In this paper we focus on associative theories of noncommutative gravity, leaving these more complicated extensions for future investigations.

Although the present paper focuses on examples of diffeomorphism-invariant field theories, it is straightforward to apply our formalism to other field theories with less symmetry by restricting to a Hopf subalgebra of vector fields over which the classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra is a module. Our approach also offers a straightforward route to “noncommutative higher gauge theory” through
k
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras with
k>4
. Along these lines, it is natural to wonder if our braided noncommutative field theories arise as suitable low energy limits of string theory, similarly to the conventional models based on star-gauge symmetry (see e.g.
[
51
]
for a review). A promising direction is to look for connections to braided symmetries of string amplitudes, for instance through the Hopf algebraic symmetries of worldsheet string correlation functions discussed in
[
2
,
1
]
. Such a connection further has the potential to connect noncommutative gravity with string theory for the first time. All of these interesting extensions of the present work are left for future investigations.

In this paper we treat only the case of classical field theories. Quantization of our braided field theories should be made possible by firstly finding a braided version of the Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra, which would then produce a braided version of the BV–BRST formalism for field theories. This should then be compared to Oeckl’s ‘braided quantum field theory’ framework
[
45
,
48
]
, which however has so far been restricted to field theories without gauge symmetries. Some steps in this direction have been discussed recently in
[
44
]
. The formalism of
[
44
]
also suggests a natural way to handle the problem of defining the braided moduli space of classical solutions, which in the dual setting of the braided BV formalism may be completely characterised by its braided differential graded algebra of functionals. These possibilities and their physical relevance
will be explored in future investigations.

2
L_{\infty}
-algebras and classical field theory

2.1
L_{\infty}
-algebras and cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras

An
L_{\infty}
-algebra is a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded real vector space
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,V_{k}
equipped with graded antisymmetric multilinear maps

\displaystyle\ell_{n}:\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V\longrightarrow V\ ,\quad v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{n}\longmapsto\ell_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})

for each
n\geq 1
, which we call
n
-brackets. The graded antisymmetry translates to

\ell_{n}(\dots,v,v^{\prime},\dots)=-(-1)^{|v|\,|v^{\prime}|}\,\ell_{n}(\dots,v^{\prime},v,\dots)\ ,

(2.1)

where we denote the degree of a homogeneous element
v\in V
by
|v|
.
The
n
-bracket is a map of degree
|\ell_{n}|=2-n
, that is

\big|\ell_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\big|=2-n+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\,|v_{j}|\ .

The
n
-brackets
\ell_{n}
are required to fulfill infinitely many
identities
{\cal J}_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=0
for each
n\geq 1
, called homotopy relations, with

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n}):=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\,(-1)^{i\,(n-i)}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{i,n-i}}\,

\displaystyle\chi(\sigma;v_{1},\dots,v_{n})

(2.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\ell_{n+1-i}\big(\ell_{i}(v_{\sigma(1)},\dots,v_{\sigma(i)}),v_{\sigma(i+1)},\dots,v_{\sigma(n)}\big)\ ,

where, for each
i=1,\dots,n
, the second sum runs over
(i,n-i)
-shuffled permutations

\sigma\in S_{n}
of degree
n
which are restricted as

\sigma(1)<\dots<\sigma(i)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sigma(i+1)<\dots<\sigma(n)\ .

The Koszul sign
\chi(\sigma;v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=\pm\,1
is determined from the grading by

\displaystyle v_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\sigma(n)}=\chi(\sigma;v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\ v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}\ .

Let us look explicitly at the first three identities. For
n=1
the
identity
{\cal J}_{1}(v)=0
is

\displaystyle\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{1}(v)\big)=0\ ,

which states that the map
\ell_{1}:V\to V
is a
differential making
(V,\ell_{1})
into a cochain complex. For
n=2
the
identity
{\cal J}_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})=0
reads

\displaystyle\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})\big)=\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(v_{1}),v_{2}\big)+(-1)^{|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(v_{1},\ell_{1}(v_{2})\big)\ ,

which states that
\ell_{1}
is a graded
derivation with respect to the
2
-bracket
\ell_{2}:V\otimes V\to V
. For
n=3
the
identity
{\cal J}_{3}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=0
expands into

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},v_{2}),v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{2}|\,|v_{3}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},v_{3}),v_{2}\big)+(-1)^{(|v_{2}|+|v_{3}|)\,|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{2},v_{3}),v_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(v_{1}),v_{2},v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{3}\big(v_{1},\ell_{1}(v_{2}),v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{1}|+|v_{2}|}\,\ell_{3}\big(v_{1},v_{2},\ell_{1}(v_{3})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{3}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\big)\ ,

(2.3)

which for
\ell_{3}=0
is just the graded Jacobi identity for the

2
-bracket
\ell_{2}
; in general it expresses the violation of the Jacobi identity by
a cochain homotopy determined by
\ell_{1}
. In this sense
L_{\infty}
-algebras are (strong) homotopy
deformations of differential graded Lie algebras which are the special
cases where the ternary and all higher brackets vanish:
\ell_{n}=0
for
all
n\geq 3
. In general, the homotopy relations for
n\geq 3
are
generalized Jacobi identities.

We are sometimes interested in the case where an

L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is further endowed with a graded symmetric non-degenerate bilinear pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
which is cyclic in the sense that

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},\ell_{n}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\rangle=(-1)^{n+(|v_{0}|+|v_{n}|)\,n+|v_{n}|\,\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\,|v_{i}|}\ \langle v_{n},\ell_{n}(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1})\rangle

for all
n\geq 1
. This is the natural notion of an invariant inner product on an

L_{\infty}
-algebra, and if such a pairing
exists the resulting algebraic structure is called a cyclic

L_{\infty}
-algebra; this generalizes the notion of a quadratic Lie algebra. If in addition the pairing is odd, say of degree

-2p-1
, then the only non-vanishing pairings are

\langle-,-\rangle:V_{k}\otimes V_{2p-k+1}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{R}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad k\leq p\ ,

so that the pairing is strictly symmetric and the cyclicity condition simplifies to

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},\ell_{n}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\rangle=(-1)^{(|v_{0}|+1)\,n}\ \langle v_{n},\ell_{n}(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1})\rangle\ .

2.2
Classical field theories in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

In this paper, by a classical field theory we will mean a polynomial (or perturbative)
field theory with irreducible gauge symmetries. They can be completely
encoded in
4
-term
L_{\infty}
-algebras concentrated in degrees

0
,
1
,
2
, and
3
:

V=V_{0}\oplus V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\oplus V_{3}\ .

The kinematical data is encoded in the vector space
V_{0}
of
gauge parameters and in the vector space
V_{1}
of fields, while the
dynamical data is encoded in
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
. These vector spaces are
typically infinite-dimensional spaces of sections of vector bundles
over a manifold
M
.

The full symmetries and dynamics of field theories are expanded in terms of the
brackets as follows.
Given
\lambda\in V_{0}
and
A\in V_{1}
, the gauge
variations are encoded as the maps
A\mapsto A+\delta_{\lambda}A
where

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}A=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n+1}(\lambda,A,\dots,A)\ ,

(2.4)

where the brackets involve
n
insertions of the field

A
.
The field equations
F_{A}=0
are encoded through

\displaystyle F_{A}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A)\ ,

(2.5)

with the covariant gauge variations

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}F_{A}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n+2}(\lambda,F_{A},A,\dots,A)\ .

(2.6)

If the distribution in
TV_{1}
spanned by the gauge parameters is
involutive off-shell, that is, when
F_{A}\neq 0
, then the homotopy relations imply that the closure relation for the gauge algebra has the form

\displaystyle[\delta_{\lambda_{1}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}]_{\circ}A=\delta_{[\![\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\!]_{A}}A\ ,

(2.7)

where
[\delta_{\lambda_{1}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}]_{\circ}:=\delta_{\lambda_{1}}\circ\delta_{\lambda_{2}}-\delta_{\lambda_{2}}\circ\delta_{\lambda_{1}}
, and

\displaystyle[\![\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\!]_{A}=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n+2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},A,\dots,A)\ .

The homotopy relations guarantee that the Jacobi
identity is generally satisfied for any triple of maps
\delta_{\lambda_{1}}
,

\delta_{\lambda_{2}}
and
\delta_{\lambda_{3}}
.
The Noether identities are encoded by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\ell_{n+1}(F_{A},A,\dots,A)=0\ ,

(2.8)

which vanishes identically as a consequence of the homotopy relations

{\cal J}_{n}(A,\dots,A)=0
, for all
n\geq 1
, of the

L_{\infty}
-algebra
[
37
]
.

For a field theory with a Lagrangian formulation, the action functional is encoded
via a symmetric non-degenerate bilinear pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
of degree
-3
, which makes
(V,\langle-,-\rangle)
into a cyclic

L_{\infty}
-algebra. Then it is easy to see that the field equations

F_{A}=0
follow from varying the action functional defined as

\displaystyle S(A):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\langle A,\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A)\rangle\ ,

(2.9)

since then cyclicity implies
\delta S(A)=\langle F_{A},\delta A\rangle
.
Cyclicity also implies

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}S(A)=\langle F_{A},\delta_{\lambda}A\rangle=-\langle{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A},\lambda\rangle\ ,

(2.10)

so that gauge invariance of the action functional
\delta_{\lambda}S(A)=0
is then equivalent to the Noether identities

{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}=0
.
For further details and the connections
to the BV–BRST formalism see
[
37
]
, while details of the
aspects covered in this paper are found in
[
30
]
.

2.3
Example I: Chern–Simons gauge theory

Let us illustrate how to reconstruct Chern–Simons theory in three
spacetime dimensions in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework, which is the prototypical example of a gauge theory with a simple
bracket structure, see e.g.
[
30
,
37
]
. Let
\mathfrak{g}

be a real Lie algebra with Lie bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}
and let
M
be a
three-manifold. Then the 4-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra which organises Chern–Simons
gauge theory on
M
is simply the differential graded Lie algebra whose underlying cochain complex is the
de Rham complex
(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),\mathrm{d})
in three dimensions with
values in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and whose underlying graded Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)
is given by extending the Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{g}
to

\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}):=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{g}
by the tensor
product with the exterior multiplication of forms; that is,

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{d}\alpha\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}(\alpha,\beta)=-[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}

for
\alpha,\beta\in V=\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.

It is easy to check that these brackets reproduce the kinematical
and dynamical content of classical Chern–Simons gauge theory via the prescription of
Section
2.2
. The transformation of a gauge field

A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
by a gauge parameter

\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}A

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(\lambda)+\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)=\mathrm{d}\lambda+[A,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ .

As for any
differential graded Lie algebra, these transformations close a (field-independent)
gauge algebra

[\![\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\!]_{A}=-\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}
, which in this case is just the Lie
algebra
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. The field equations
F_{A}=0
are encoded by

\displaystyle F_{A}

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}(A,A)=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ ,

with

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}F_{A}

\displaystyle=\ell_{2}(\lambda,F_{A})=[F_{A},\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}

expressing the covariance of the
curvature 2-forms
F_{A}\in\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})
under local gauge
transformations. Thus the field equations
F_{A}=0
are solved by flat
connections of a trivial principal bundle on the three-manifold

M
, which are locally gauge equivalent to the trivial connection
A=0
. Finally

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(F_{A})-\ell_{2}(A,F_{A})=\mathrm{d}F_{A}+[A,F_{A}]_{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathrm{d}^{A}F_{A}\ ,

where
\mathrm{d}^{A}
denotes the usual covariant derivative. Thus the Noether
identities
{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}=0
are equivalent to the Bianchi identities

\mathrm{d}^{A}F_{A}=0
in
\Omega^{3}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. In this way we reproduce the usual
space of classical physical states of Chern–Simons theory as the moduli
space of flat connections on
M
.

To reproduce the Chern–Simons action functional in this
formulation, we need further structure: we assume that
M
is a closed
oriented three-manifold and that
\mathfrak{g}
is a quadratic Lie algebra. We
may then construct a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
V
by
defining the pairing of
\mathfrak{g}
-valued forms in complementary degrees:

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\ ,

(2.11)

where
|\alpha|+|\beta|=3
. This defines a cyclic non-degenerate pairing of
degree
-3
, where cyclicity follows from the invariance of the
quadratic form
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbbm{R}
on
\mathfrak{g}
.
Then the Chern–Simons action
functional is reproduced according to the general
prescription of Section
2.2
:

\displaystyle S(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}(A)\rangle-\frac{1}{6}\,\langle A,\ell_{2}(A,A)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(A\wedge\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{3}\,A\wedge[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big)\ .

(2.12)

2.4
Example II: Einstein–Cartan–Palatini gravity

We shall now review the reconstruction of the first-order
formulation of gravity in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework, from the
perspective of the Einstein–Cartan–Palatini (ECP) formalism for
general relativity,
following
[
30
]
. To set up notation and to illustrate the ideas, here we only
consider the
L_{\infty}
-algebras in three spacetime dimensions and in Euclidean
signature. The general cases of arbitrary dimensionality and signature
are treated in detail in
[
30
]
, and later we shall also
deal with the four-dimensional case. We comment below on the
differences when one extends these constructions to higher dimensions
(independently of the signature of spacetime, see
[
30
]
).

Let
M
be a parallelizable three-manifold; for this it suffices to
suppose that
M
is orientable, a condition we will need anyway later on. The graded vector space

V
underlying the
L_{\infty}
-algebra which organises ECP gravity on
M
is given
by

\displaystyle V:=V_{0}\oplus V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\oplus V_{3}

(2.13)

where

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(TM)\times\Omega^{0}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(3)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{1}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3})\times\Omega^{1}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(3)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\big(M,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathbbm{R}^{3}\big)\times\Omega^{2}\big(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{1}\big(M,\Omega^{3}(M)\big)\times\Omega^{3}\big(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3}\big)\ .

In the following we denote elements in degrees
0
,
1
,
2
and
3

respectively by
(\xi,\rho)\in V_{0}
,

(e,\omega)\in V_{1}
,
(E,{\Omega})\in V_{2}
,
and
({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{P}})\in V_{3}
.
This yields a 4-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra with differential defined by

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\xi,\rho)=(0,\mathrm{d}\rho)\ ,\quad\ell_{1}(e,\omega)=(\mathrm{d}\omega,\mathrm{d}e)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}(E,{\Omega})=(0,\mathrm{d}{\Omega})\ .

The
2
-brackets are defined by

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big((\xi_{1},\rho_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\rho_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\big([\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}\,,\,-[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}\rho_{2}-\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{2}}\rho_{1}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(-\rho\cdot e+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}e\,,\,-[\rho,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\omega\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(-[\rho,E]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}E\,,\,-\rho\cdot{\Omega}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}{\Omega}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{P}})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\rho\curlywedge{\mathcal{P}})+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}{\mathcal{X}}\,,\,-\rho\cdot{\mathcal{P}}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}{\mathcal{P}}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big((e_{1},\omega_{1})\,,\,(e_{2},\omega_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=-\big([\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}\,,\,\omega_{1}\wedge e_{2}+\omega_{2}\wedge e_{1}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge E+\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\omega\curlywedge{\Omega}-\iota_{\mu}e\curlywedge\mathrm{d}E-\iota_{\mu}\omega\curlywedge\mathrm{d}{\Omega})\,,

\displaystyle\phantom{{}(\mathrm{Tr}\big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge E+{}}E\wedge e-\omega\wedge{\Omega}\big)\ ,

(2.14)

while all the rest of the brackets vanish. As shown
in
[
30
]
, this defines a differential graded Lie algebra.

Let us explain the notation used, as well as the content of these
brackets following from the general prescription of Section
2.2
. The
dynamical fields of ECP gravity on
M
are pairs
(e,\omega)

consisting of a coframe field
e\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3})

and a spin connection
\omega\in\Omega^{1}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(3)\big)
. Using the isomorphism

\mathfrak{so}(3)\cong\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathbbm{R}^{3}
, one identifies the
spin connection as an element
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(M,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathbbm{R}^{3})
; we may then expand
e=e^{a}\,{\tt E}_{a}
and
\omega=\omega^{ab}\,{\tt E}_{a}\wedge{\tt E}_{b}
, where
e^{a}
and
\omega^{ab}=-\omega^{ba}
are
1
-forms on
M
, and
{\tt E}_{a}
for
a=1,2,3
form the standard orthonormal basis of
\mathbbm{R}^{3}
. The
\curlywedge
-product separately takes the
exterior products of the ‘curved’ spacetime differential forms on the
manifold
M

and ‘flat’ spacetime multivectors on the vector space
\mathbbm{R}^{3}
; in particular,
\omega\curlywedge e=\omega^{ab}\wedge e^{c}\,{\tt E}_{a}\wedge{\tt E}_{b}\wedge{\tt E}_{c}
while
\omega\wedge e=\delta_{bc}\,\omega^{ab}\,\wedge e^{c}\,{\tt E}_{a}
. Then

\mathrm{Tr}:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{3}\mathbbm{R}^{3}\to\mathbbm{R}
is the Hodge duality
operator on
\mathbbm{R}^{3}
endowed with the standard Euclidean metric, defined by
\mathrm{Tr}({\tt E}_{a}\wedge{\tt E}_{b}\wedge{\tt E}_{c})=\epsilon_{abc}
.

The gauge parameters are pairs
(\xi,\rho)
consisting of a vector field

\xi\in\Gamma(TM)
and a local rotation

\rho\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{so}(3))
. Following the prescription of
Section
2.2
, their action on fields is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}(e,\omega)=\ell_{1}(\xi,\rho)+\ell_{2}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)=(-\rho\cdot e+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}e\,,\,\mathrm{d}\rho-[\rho,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\omega)

where
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}
is the Lie derivative along the vector field
\xi
, and

\rho\cdot e=\rho^{a}{}_{b}\,e^{b}\,{\tt E}_{a}
denotes matrix multiplication in the fundamental
representation of
\mathfrak{so}(3)
.
Thus
\xi
generates the standard diffeomorphism symmetry of
general relativity, while
\rho
generates changes of
orthonormal coframes for a given metric. The gauge algebra

\displaystyle[\![(\xi_{1},\rho_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\rho_{2})]\!]_{(e,\omega)}=-\ell_{2}\big((\xi_{1},\rho_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\rho_{2})\big)=\big(-[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}\,,\,[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}-\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}\rho_{2}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{2}}\rho_{1}\big)

is the semi-direct product of the Lie algebras of vector fields on
M

and of local rotations:

\displaystyle\Gamma(TM)\ltimes\Omega^{0}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(3)\big)\ .

(2.15)

We denote the Lie bracket of vector fields on
\mathfrak{v}:=\Gamma(TM)
by

[-,-]_{\mathfrak{v}}
.

The field equations are given by

\displaystyle F_{(e,\omega)}=\ell_{1}(e,\omega)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)=(\mathrm{d}\omega,\mathrm{d}e)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,([\omega,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)},2\,\omega\wedge e)=(R,T)\ ,

where

\displaystyle R:=\mathrm{d}\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\omega,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}\ \in\ \Omega^{2}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(3)\big)

is the curvature
2
-form of the spin connection
\omega
, while the
covariant derivative of the coframe field

\displaystyle T:=\mathrm{d}^{\omega}e=\mathrm{d}e+\omega\wedge e\,\,\,\in\,\,\,\Omega^{2}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3})

is the torsion
2
-form. The field equations
F_{(e,\omega)}=(0,0)
thus
imply two conditions: Firstly, that the torsion vanishes,
T=0
, which for non-degenerate coframe
fields
e
can be solved to identify
\omega
with the Levi–Civita
connection of the metric
\delta^{ab}\,e_{a}\otimes e_{b}
. Secondly, that
the curvature vanishes,
R=0
, which is just the vacuum Einstein field
equation in three dimensions, whose solutions correspond to flat
spacetimes which are locally isometric to Euclidean space. In this way
we can recover the usual space of classical physical states of
three-dimensional general relativity (in the absence of matter fields). However,
the ECP theory makes sense for degenerate coframes
e
, and this
extension of general relativity is necessary for its

L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation where the space of fields is required
to be a vector (or an affine) space. The covariance of the field
equations is expressed through

\displaystyle\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}F_{(e,\omega)}=\ell_{2}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,F_{(e,\omega)}\big)=(-[\rho,R]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}R,-\rho\cdot T+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}T)\ .

Finally,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{(e,\omega)}F_{(e,\omega)}

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(F_{(e,\omega)})-\ell_{2}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,F_{(e,\omega)}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}(\iota_{\mu}e\curlywedge\mathrm{d}R-\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge R+\iota_{\mu}\omega\curlywedge\mathrm{d}T-\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\omega\curlywedge T)\,,\,\mathrm{d}^{\omega}T-R\wedge e\big)\ ,

where
\iota_{\mu}
denotes the contraction with vectors

\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
of the
local holonomic frame dual to the basis
\{\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\}
of
1
-forms
in a local coordinate chart on
M
, and
we identify the vector space of
1
-forms valued in
3
-forms
\Omega^{1}\big(M,\Omega^{3}(M)\big)
with

\Omega^{1}(M)\otimes\Omega^{3}(M)
. The Noether identities

{\mathsf{d}}_{(e,\omega)}F_{(e,\omega)}=(0,0)
thus impose two differential
identities among the field equations, the second of which
is equivalent to the first Bianchi identity
\mathrm{d}^{\omega}T=R\wedge e
.

The action functional can be derived in this language by assuming
M

is closed
5
5

5

Alternatively, we may work with fields of suitable asymptotic decay.
and defining a
cyclic pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
of
degree
-3
as
[
30
]

\displaystyle\langle(e,\omega)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\rangle:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(e\curlywedge E+{\Omega}\curlywedge\omega\big)\ ,

(2.16)

on
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
, and as

\displaystyle\langle(\xi,\rho)\,,\,({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{P}})\rangle:=\int_{M}\,\iota_{\xi}{\mathcal{X}}+\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\rho\curlywedge{\mathcal{P}}\big)\ ,

(2.17)

on
V_{0}\otimes V_{3}
, where
\iota_{\xi}
denotes contraction with the
vector field
\xi
. Then the prescription of
Section
2.2
yields the standard ECP action functional
in three dimensions:

\displaystyle S(e,\omega)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big\langle(e,\omega)\,,\,\ell_{1}(e,\omega)\big\rangle-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big\langle(e,\omega)\,,\,\ell_{2}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\Big(e\curlywedge\big(\mathrm{d}\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\omega,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(e\curlywedge R\big)=\int_{M}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,e^{a}\wedge R^{bc}\ .

(2.18)

The first Noether identity from
{\mathsf{d}}_{(e,\omega)}F_{(e,\omega)}=(0,0)
, in

\Omega^{1}(M,\Omega^{3}(M))
,
corresponds to local diffeomorphism invariance

\delta_{(\xi,0)}S(e,\omega)=0
, while the second Noether identity, in
\Omega^{3}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3})
, corresponds to the local gauge symmetry

\delta_{(0,\rho)}S(e,\omega)=0
. This construction can be easily
extended to incorporate a cosmological constant
[
30
]
, and
we will do so later on when we study the braided version of this theory.

In higher dimensions
d\geq 4
, the
L_{\infty}
-algebras which organise ECP gravity contain
higher brackets and are no longer simply differential graded Lie
algebras, owing to the higher degree polynomial nature of the theory
in the coframes. We will consider the four-dimensional case in detail
later on, where the theory is again classically equivalent to general
relativity, but now with propagating degrees of freedom.

3
Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization

3.1
Drinfel’d twists on manifolds

We briefly summarise the basic theory of Drinfel’d
twists
[
43
]
, specialised from the outset to the Lie
algebra of vector fields
\mathfrak{v}:=\Gamma(TM)
on a manifold
M
, which
generate infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of
M
; here we regard
\mathfrak{v}

as a Lie algebra over
\mathbbm{C}
(or more precisely its complexification
\mathfrak{v}\otimes\mathbbm{C}
). The main object of
interest in this theory is the universal enveloping algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
of

\mathfrak{v}
, which is the tensor algebra (over
\mathbbm{C}
) of
\mathfrak{v}
,
regarded as the free unital algebra generated by
\mathfrak{v}
,
modulo the two-sided ideal generated by
\xi_{1}\,\xi_{2}-\xi_{2}\,\xi_{1}-[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}
for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{v}
.

The enveloping algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
is naturally a cocommutative Hopf
algebra with coproduct
\Delta:U\mathfrak{v}\to U\mathfrak{v}\otimes U\mathfrak{v}
, counit

\varepsilon:U\mathfrak{v}\to\mathbbm{C}
and antipode
S:U\mathfrak{v}\to U\mathfrak{v}
defined on
generators by

\displaystyle\Delta(\xi)

\displaystyle=\xi\otimes 1+1\otimes\xi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Delta(1)=1\otimes 1\ ,

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\xi)

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon(1)=1\ ,

\displaystyle S(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\xi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S(1)=1\ ,

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
. The maps
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are
extended as algebra homomorphisms, and
S
as an algebra
antihomomorphism to all of
U\mathfrak{v}
. We adopt the standard
Sweedler notation
\Delta(X)=:X_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\otimes X_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}
(with summations understood) to abbreviate the
coproduct of
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
.

From the perspective of field theory, the Hopf algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
is
generally “too big” for the purposes of deformation quantization, as the
ensuing constructions would then only work for
diffeomorphism-invariant field theories. This will actually be the case for the
examples treated in this paper, but to handle other field theories
with a smaller set of spacetime symmetries, one could simply replace

\mathfrak{v}
with any Lie subalgebra and apply all of the following
constructions to the corresponding enveloping Hopf subalgebra
\mathcal{H}\subseteq U\mathfrak{v}
. This can be interpreted as performing the deformation
quantization along the symmetries of
M
which leave the field
theory on
M
invariant, see for instance
[
25
,
55
,
22
,
33
]
for examples.

To treat the typical examples of twists which arise in physics, we
shall need to introduce formal power series extensions in a
deformation parameter
\hbar
. If
V
is a complex vector space, we
denote by
V[[\hbar]]
the vector space of formal power series in

\hbar
with coefficients in
V
; it is naturally a module over

\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
. If
V
and
W
are complex vector spaces, then

V[[\hbar]]\otimes W[[\hbar]]\cong(V\otimes W)[[\hbar]]
, where on the
left-hand side we use the appropriate topological tensor product (see
e.g.
[
16
]
for further details), which for
simplicity we do not distinguish from the usual tensor product of
vector spaces over
\mathbbm{C}
. With these conventions, we denote by

U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
the formal power series extension of the
cocommutative Hopf algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
, with operations applied term by term to the coefficients of series.

A
Drinfel’d twist
is a normalized
2
-cocycle of the Hopf
algebra
U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
. By this we mean an invertible element

\mathcal{F}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
satisfying the cocycle
condition

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{12}\,(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{23}\,(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathcal{F}\ ,

where
\mathcal{F}_{12}=\mathcal{F}\otimes 1
and
\mathcal{F}_{23}=1\otimes\mathcal{F}
, together
with the normalization condition

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}=1=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\mathcal{F}\ .

We write the power series expansion of the twist as
\mathcal{F}=:{\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, with the sum over

k
understood. Then the cocycle
condition may be written in Sweedler notation as

\displaystyle{\sf f}^{k}\,{\sf f}^{l}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\,{\sf f}^{l}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\otimes{\sf f}_{l}={\sf f}^{l}\otimes{\sf f}^{k}\,{{\sf f}_{l}}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\,{{\sf f}_{l}}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\ ,

(3.1)

and the normalization condition as

\displaystyle\varepsilon({\sf f}^{k})\,{\sf f}_{k}=1={\sf f}^{k}\,\varepsilon({\sf f}_{k})\ .

As a consequence, the inverse twist
\mathcal{F}^{-1}=:\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
satisfies
similar conditions.

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
defines a new Hopf algebra structure on the
universal enveloping algebra
U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, which we denote by

U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
. As algebras,
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}=U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
and also the
counit of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
is the same as the counit
\varepsilon
of

U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
. The new coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
and antipode
S_{\mathcal{F}}

of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X):=\mathcal{F}\,\Delta(X)\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S_{\mathcal{F}}(X):={\sf f}^{k}\,S({\sf f}_{k})\,S(X)\,S(\bar{{\sf f}}^{l})\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{l}\ ,

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
. For
X\in U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
, we adopt the Sweedler notation
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X)=:X_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\otimes X_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}
to distinguish the twisted and untwisted coproducts.

This new Hopf algebra is not cocommutative in general,
i.e.
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}\neq\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm op}:=\tau\circ\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
, where

\tau
is the transposition which interchanges the factors in a tensor
product. However, the cocommutativity is controlled up to a

braiding
given by the invertible
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix
{\mathcal{R}}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
induced by the twist as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}=:\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}\ ,

(3.2)

where
\mathcal{F}_{21}=\tau(\mathcal{F})={\sf f}_{k}\otimes{\sf f}^{k}
is the twist with its legs
swapped. Explicitly

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm op}(X)={\mathcal{R}}\,\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X)\,{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\ .

It is easy to see that the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is triangular, that is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathsf{R}_{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}\ ,

and moreover that

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mathrm{id}){\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{23}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}){\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\ ,

where
{\mathcal{R}}_{13}=\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes 1\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}
, or in Sweedler notation

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{k}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}=\mathsf{R}^{l}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}=\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\ .

(3.3)

The
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix also satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{23}={\mathcal{R}}_{23}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\ .

(3.4)

Example 3.5
.

The standard example on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{d}
is the
abelian Hermitian Moyal–Weyl twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\exp\big(-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\otimes\partial_{\nu}\big)=:{\sf f}^{k}_{\theta}\otimes{\sf f}_{\theta k}\ ,

(3.6)

where
(\theta^{\mu\nu})
is a
d{\times}d
antisymmetric real-valued
matrix. This twist is based on the enveloping Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}
of
the abelian Lie algebra of infinitesimal translations. In this case the twisted Hopf
algebra is cocommutative (in fact
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\theta}}=\Delta
), and the

{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\theta}=\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{-2}=\exp\big(\,\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\otimes\partial_{\nu}\big)\ .

However, our
considerations in the following apply to a more general class of
Drinfel’d twists that we shall specify more precisely later on.

3.2
Modules

Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization consists in twisting the enveloping
Hopf algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
to a non-cocommutative Hopf algebra
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
,
while simultaneously twisting all of its modules
[
13
]
. There is a symmetric
monoidal category
\mathscr{M}
whose objects are (left)

U\mathfrak{v}
-modules and whose morphisms are equivariant maps (see
e.g.
[
16
]
). Since
U\mathfrak{v}
is a cocommutative Hopf
algebra (equivalently it has a triangular structure with trivial

{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix
1\otimes 1
), the braiding isomorphism of
\mathscr{M}
is just the trivial
transposition
\tau
.

A
U\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra is an algebra in the category

\mathscr{M}
. By this we mean an algebra
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
with a

U\mathfrak{v}
-action
\triangleright:U\mathfrak{v}\otimes\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
which is compatible with the algebra multiplication
via the coproduct
\Delta
, that is

\displaystyle X\triangleright\mu(a\otimes b)=\mu\big(\Delta(X)\triangleright(a\otimes b)\big)

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, where
\mu:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

is the product on
\mathcal{A}
. We will usually drop the symbol

\triangleright
to simplify the notation. This condition means in particular that
vector fields
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
act on
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
as derivations:

\displaystyle\xi\big(\mu(a\otimes b)\big)=\mu\big(\xi(a)\otimes b\big)+\mu\big(a\otimes\xi(b)\big)\ .

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
defines a functorially equivalent symmetric monoidal
category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}
of left

U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules
[
16
]
. The braiding isomorphism of

{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}
is now non-trivial and given by composing the transposition

\tau
with the action of the inverse of the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix. Since
{\mathcal{R}}
is
triangular,
{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}
, the braiding is symmetric,
i.e. the braiding isomorphism squares to the identity
morphism. The action of the inverse
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix on arbitrary tensor products of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules
\mathcal{V}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{V}_{n}
can be computed using the identities (
3.3
) for
{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}
and their iterations to get

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n-1})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}(v_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}}(v_{n-1})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(v_{n})\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}}(v_{1})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(v_{2})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}}(v_{n})\ ,

(3.7)

for
n\geq 2
and
v_{i}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Intuitively, the identities (
3.2
) tell us that passing an element “at once" over many elements
v_{i}
is the same as passing successively over “each one” individually.

If
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
is a (left)
U\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra, then we
can deform the product
\mu
on
\mathcal{A}
by precomposing it with the
inverse of the twist

\mathcal{F}
to get a new product

\displaystyle\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)=\mu\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(a\otimes b)=\mu\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k}(a)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(b)\big)\ ,

(3.8)

for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, where on the right-hand side we extend
\mu
to

\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]]\cong(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})[[\hbar]]
by
applying it term by term to the coefficients of a formal power series.
The cocycle condition on
\mathcal{F}
guarantees that this
produces an associative star-product
\mu_{\star}
on
\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]]
, and it generally defines a
noncommutative
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra
(\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]],\mu_{\star})
,
that is, an algebra
in the category of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules:

\displaystyle X\big(\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)\big)=\mu_{\star}\big(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X)(a\otimes b)\big)\ ,

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
.
In the following we denote
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
by
\mathcal{A}
and

(\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]],\mu_{\star})
by
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
for brevity.

If the algebra
\mathcal{A}
is commutative,
then
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
is braided commutative: the noncommutativity of

\mathcal{A}_{\star}
is controlled by the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as

\displaystyle\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)=\mu_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(b)\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}(a)\big)\ ,

which is easily proven by recalling that
{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}
.

Example 3.9
.

We apply this construction to
Example
3.5
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the commutative algebra of smooth
complex-valued functions
on
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, with the usual pointwise multiplication

\mu(f\otimes g)=f\,g
. Then we obtain the noncommutative Moyal–Weyl star-product

\displaystyle f\star_{\theta}g

\displaystyle=\mu\circ\mathcal{F}_{\theta}^{-1}(f\otimes g)

\displaystyle={\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}}^{k}(f)\,{\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}}{}_{k}(g)

\displaystyle=f\,g+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\mathrm{i}\,\hbar)^{k}}{2^{k}\,k!}\,\theta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\cdots\theta^{\mu_{k}\nu_{k}}\,\partial_{\mu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\mu_{k}}f\,\partial_{\nu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\nu_{k}}g

of smooth functions
f
and
g
on
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
.

The tensor algebra of differential forms and vector fields on the manifold
M
is covariant under the action
of the universal enveloping algebra of infinitesimal diffeomorphisms

U\mathfrak{v}
. We can construct a noncommutative differential geometry on

M
by requiring it to be covariant with respect to the twisted Hopf
algebra
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}

[
13
,
11
]
. We illustrate this
below by considering the deformation quantization of the exterior
algebra of differential forms and the (Lie and universal enveloping)
algebra of vector fields. Drinfel’d twist deformation of differential and Cartan calculus on a manifold
M
has been studied in
[
12
,
11
,
54
,
3
]
.

3.3
Differential forms

Let
\mathcal{A}=\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M),\wedge\big)
be the exterior
algebra of differential forms on
M
(or
more precisely its complexification). This is a module over the Lie
algebra of vector fields
\mathfrak{v}
, where the (left) action is given by
the Lie derivative:

\displaystyle\xi(\alpha):=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\alpha

for
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
. This action is
extended to a
U\mathfrak{v}
-action via successive applications of the Lie
derivative, viewed as higher order differential operators:

\displaystyle\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}(\alpha)=(\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{n}})\alpha

for
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}\in\mathfrak{v}
. This makes

\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M),\wedge\big)
into a
U\mathfrak{v}
-module
algebra, since

\displaystyle\xi(\alpha\wedge\beta)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\alpha\wedge\beta+\alpha\wedge\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\beta=\wedge\circ\Delta(\xi)(\alpha\otimes\beta)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
.

The twisted exterior algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\star}=\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M)[[\hbar]],\wedge_{\star}\big)
is a

U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra. Following the prescription
(
3.8
), for
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
we set

\displaystyle\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta:=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\alpha)\wedge\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\beta)\ ,

with (graded) braided commutativity controlled by the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as

\displaystyle\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta=(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\ \mathsf{R}_{k}(\beta)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\alpha)\ .

The standard (undeformed) exterior derivative

\mathrm{d}:\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
is then an ordinary (not
braided!) graded derivation of the twisted exterior algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta)=\mathrm{d}\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|}\,\alpha\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\beta\ .

This property is fulfilled because the usual exterior derivative
commutes with the Lie derivatives that enter in the definition of the
star-exterior product. It is also still a differential on
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
,
that is,
\mathrm{d}^{2}=0
. This twists the de Rham complex
(\Omega^{\bullet}(M),\mathrm{d})
of the
manifold
M
to a noncommutative differential graded algebra
(\mathcal{A}_{\star},\mathrm{d})
.

In our applications to field theory, we shall be interested in a special
class of Drinfel’d twists. If
M
is a closed oriented manifold of
dimension
d
, we will require that the usual integral over
M
is graded
cyclic under the deformed exterior
product
\wedge_{\star}
:

\displaystyle\int_{M}\,\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta=(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\int_{M}\,\beta\wedge_{\star}\alpha\ ,

(3.10)

where
|\alpha|+|\beta|=d
. This is guaranteed if the twist
\mathcal{F}

satisfies the condition

S(\bar{\sf f}^{k})\,\bar{\sf f}_{k}=1
, where
S
is the antipode of

U\mathfrak{v}

[
4
]
; for example, this holds for abelian twists
6
6

6

That is, a Drinfel’d twist generated by an abelian subalgebra of
\mathfrak{v}
, such as the Moyal–Weyl twist of Example
3.5
.
, whereby
\mathcal{F}_{21}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}
. We shall further
require that the twist
\mathcal{F}
is Hermitian, that is, it defines a
Hermitian star-product
\wedge_{\star}
:

\displaystyle(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta)^{*}=(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta^{*}\wedge_{\star}\alpha^{*}\ ,

where
∗
denotes complex conjugation. This is guaranteed if
\mathcal{F}

satisfies the reality condition given by
\bar{\sf f}^{k}{}^{*}\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}{}^{*}=S(\bar{\sf f}_{k})\otimes S(\bar{\sf f}^{k})
.

All of this has an extension to the graded Lie algebra

\mathcal{A}=(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}})
of differential forms valued
in a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, where
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}
denotes the tensor product
of exterior multiplication of forms with the Lie bracket of

\mathfrak{g}
. With the trivial
U\mathfrak{v}
-action on
\mathfrak{g}
, we use (
3.8
) to define

\displaystyle[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}:=[\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\alpha),\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\beta)]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ ,

for
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.
This makes

\mathcal{A}_{\star}=(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})
into a (graded)

braided Lie algebra
, that is, a graded Lie algebra in the category

{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}

[
17
]
. The new bracket
is now (graded) braided antisymmetric:

\displaystyle[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=-(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|\,|\alpha_{2}|}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\alpha_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\alpha_{1})]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

and satisfies the (graded) braided Jacobi identity:

\displaystyle[\alpha_{1},[\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=[[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star},\alpha_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|\,|\alpha_{2}|}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\alpha_{2}),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\alpha_{1}),\alpha_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(3.11)

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
; this will follow as a special example of Proposition
4.8
below.
Graded cyclicity can then be formulated
as above by precomposing the integral over
M
with an invariant
quadratic form
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

3.4
Diffeomorphisms

Since we are also interested in diffeomorphism symmetry in our
applications to gravity, we have to consider the Drinfel’d twist
deformation of the algebra of vector fields

\mathfrak{v}=\Gamma(TM)
. Deforming the Lie bracket following the general
prescription (
3.8
), we obtain the star-bracket of two
vector fields
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Gamma(TM)
as

\displaystyle[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}=[\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\xi_{1}),\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\xi_{2})]_{\mathfrak{v}}=\xi_{1}\star\xi_{2}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\xi_{1})\ ,

where the second equality takes place in the twisting of the universal
enveloping algebra of vector fields
U\mathfrak{v}
. The star-bracket makes

(\Gamma(TM)[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star})
into the braided Lie algebra
of vector fields. The star-bracket is braided antisymmetric:

\displaystyle[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}=-[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\xi_{1})]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\ ,

and it also fulfills the braided Jacobi identity

\displaystyle\big[\xi_{1},[\xi_{2},\xi_{3}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\big]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}=\big[[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star},\xi_{3}\big]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}+\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2}),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\xi_{1}),\xi_{3}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\big]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\ ,

for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\Gamma(TM)
.

Braided diffeomorphisms act via the star-Lie derivative, which is defined following (
3.8
) as

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}T=\mathrm{L}_{\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\xi)}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(T)\ ,

for any vector field
\xi
and any tensor field
T
on
M
. It provides a
representation of the braided Lie algebra of vector fields on
differential forms and tensor fields, since it
satisfies

\displaystyle[\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star},\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{2}}^{\star}]_{\circ}^{\star}:=\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star}\circ\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{2}}^{\star}-\mathrm{L}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})}^{\star}\circ\mathrm{L}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\xi_{1})}^{\star}=\mathrm{L}_{[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}}^{\star}\ .

It is also a
braided derivation
, that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}(T_{1}\otimes_{\star}T_{2})=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}T_{1}\otimes_{\star}T_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(T_{1})\otimes_{\star}\mathrm{L}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\xi)}^{\star}T_{2}\ ,

for any vector field
\xi
and any two tensors
T_{1},T_{2}
on
M
, where

T_{1}\otimes_{\star}T_{2}:=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(T_{1})\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M)}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(T_{2})
. These properties of the star-Lie derivative will be generalized to more general braided Lie algebra actions in Section
5
.

4
Braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras

4.1
L_{\infty}
-algebras in braided representation categories

Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra as defined in
Section
2.1
. The key to defining our notion of a braided

L_{\infty}
-algebra consists of reinterpreting all the defining
relations in terms of morphisms in a suitable category, before acting
on elements of
V
. This reformulation also has the advantage of
absorbing the cumbersome sign factors which arise from gradings in the
homotopy relations of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

To illustrate
the idea, let us begin by considering the simplest case where only the
2
-bracket

\ell_{2}
is non-vanishing. Let
\mathscr{V}
be the symmetric monoidal
category of vector spaces (sitting in degree
0
), whose braiding isomorphism
is given by transposition
\tau
which exchanges factors of a tensor
product of two vector spaces. Then symmetric and antisymmetric morphisms in
\mathscr{V}
are
defined by acting with the symmetric group
S_{n}
of degree
n
via
the braiding
\tau
. As an object in
\mathscr{V}
, the vector space
V

carries the structure of a Lie algebra: the morphism
\ell_{2}:V\otimes V\to V
is antisymmetric:

\displaystyle\ell_{2}=-\ell_{2}\circ\sigma_{(12)}=-\ell_{2}\circ\tau^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}_{V,V}\ ,

where
\sigma_{(12)}\in S_{2}
is the non-identity permutation of
degree
2
; on elements this reads

\displaystyle\ell_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=-\ell_{2}\big(\tau^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}_{V,V}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})\big)=-\ell_{2}(v_{2}\otimes v_{1})\ .

The Jacobi
identity for
\ell_{2}
in this framework is
\mathcal{J}_{3}=0
, where the Jacobiator is
the morphism
\mathcal{J}_{3}:V\otimes V\otimes V\to V
in
\mathscr{V}

defined via elements
\sigma\in S_{3}
permuting the entries in
V\otimes V\otimes V
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{3}:=\ell_{2}\circ(\ell_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\big(\sigma_{\mathrm{id}}+\sigma_{(132)}-\sigma_{(23)}\big)=\ell_{2}\circ(\ell_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\big(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes 3}+\tau^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}_{V,V\otimes V}-\mathrm{id}\otimes\tau^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}_{V,V}\big)\ .

(4.1)

It is easy to check on elements of
V
that
\mathcal{J}_{3}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=0
is
just the standard Jacobi identity for the
2
-bracket
\ell_{2}
.

Let now
\mathscr{V}^{\sharp}
be the symmetric monoidal
category of
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded vector spaces, whose braiding
isomorphism is given by transposition times the degree swap
multiplication, which we denote by
\tau^{\sharp}
. Now symmetric and
antisymmetric morphisms in
\mathscr{V}^{\sharp}
are defined using the
braiding
\tau^{\sharp}
to act with the symmetric group
S_{n}
, and
yield graded versions. As an object in
\mathscr{V}^{\sharp}
, the vector space

V
thus carries the structure of a graded Lie algebra: the morphism

\ell_{2}:V\otimes V\to V
of degree
0
is (graded) antisymmetric:

\displaystyle\ell_{2}=-\ell_{2}\circ\sigma_{(12)}=-\ell_{2}\circ\tau_{V,V}^{\sharp}\ ,

which on homogeneous elements reads

\displaystyle\ell_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=-\ell_{2}\big(\tau_{V,V}^{\sharp}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})\big)=-(-1)^{|v_{1}|\,|v_{2}|}\,\ell_{2}(v_{2}\otimes v_{1})\ .

The graded
Jacobi identity for
\ell_{2}
in this language is analogous to the vanishing of the
Jacobiator (
4.1
), with
\tau
replaced by

\tau^{\sharp}
. We have already encountered a concrete example
in Section
3.3
: in the present context,

(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}})
is a Lie algebra in the
representation category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules,
with
U\mathfrak{v}
itself regarded as sitting in degree
0
.

It should now be clear how to pass to the definition of a braided Lie
algebra: one simply requires a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
to act via
a (graded) braided transposition in a braided monoidal category. This can be defined in any symmetric
monoidal category, but for concreteness, and with any eye to the
applications we are interested in later on, we specialise immediately
to the twisted representation category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of

\mathbbm{Z}
-graded left
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules. Similarly to
Section
3.3
, the braiding isomorphism in this
category is determined by the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}:={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\circ\tau^{\sharp}
. Then as an
object in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, the vector space
V
carries the
structure of a braided Lie algebra: the morphism
\ell_{2}:V\otimes V\to V
satisfies

\displaystyle\ell_{2}=-\ell_{2}\circ\sigma_{(12)}=-\ell_{2}\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau_{V,V}^{\sharp}\ ,

which on homogeneous elements reads

\displaystyle\ell_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=-\ell_{2}\big({}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau_{V,V}^{\sharp}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})\big)=-(-1)^{|v_{1}|\,|v_{2}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{1})\big)\ .

The braided Jacobi identity can be expressed as in
(
4.1
) by replacing
\tau
with
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}
. In
Section
3.3
we already encountered the concrete
example of the Lie algebra

(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})
in the symmetric
monoidal category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
.

At this stage it is straightforward to pass from Lie algebras to

L_{\infty}
-algebras, allowing other brackets
\ell_{n}
to be
non-zero for
n\neq 2
. An
L_{\infty}
-algebra consists of an object
V

in the category
\mathscr{V}^{\sharp}
together with morphisms
\ell_{n}:\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V\rightarrow V
which are
antisymmetric in
\mathscr{V}^{\sharp}
. The key point is that the homotopy
relations (
2.2
) can now be expressed, without acting
explicitly on elements of
V
, as the vanishing of
morphisms
\mathcal{J}_{n}:\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V\to V
in
\mathscr{V}^{\sharp}

defined by

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{n}:=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\,(-1)^{i\,(n-i)}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{i,n-i}}\,\text{sgn}(\sigma)\ \ell_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)\circ\sigma\ ,

(4.2)

where the permutations
\sigma
act via the graded transposition

\tau^{\sharp}
on

\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V
, and
{\rm sgn}(\sigma)
is the sign of

\sigma
. It is easy to check that on elements
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V

this coincides with (
2.2
).

This leads us to the central mathematical concept of this paper.

Definition 4.3
.

A
braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
in the
symmetric monoidal category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
.

This definition simply amounts to letting the symmetric
group act in all relations of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra, written in terms
of morphisms, through the
braiding isomorphism

{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}
of

{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
; we write
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma
to distinguish the action of a permutation
\sigma
via the braided transposition from the usual one. Explicitly, as in the case of Lie algebras, this
gives the braided antisymmetry of the morphisms
\ell_{n}:\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V\to V
in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
:

\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\dots,v,v^{\prime},\dots)=-(-1)^{|v|\,|v^{\prime}|}\,\ell_{n}\big(\dots,\mathsf{R}_{k}(v^{\prime}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v),\dots\big)\ ,

(4.4)

together with
braided homotopy relations

\mathcal{J}_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=0
in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
which follow from
(
4.2
). The first two relations
\mathcal{J}_{1}(v)=0
and

\mathcal{J}_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})=0
are unchanged from the classical case of Section
2.1
:

\displaystyle\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{1}(v)\big)

\displaystyle=0\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(v_{1}),v_{2}\big)+(-1)^{|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(v_{1},\ell_{1}(v_{2})\big)\ ,

(4.5)

hence the map
\ell_{1}:V\to V
is still a differential which is a
graded (but not braided!) derivation of the
2
-bracket

\ell_{2}:V\otimes V\to V
. The first homotopy relation which differs
from the classical case is

\mathcal{J}_{3}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=0
, which is a deformation of the classical
identity (
2.3
) in accordance with the non-trivial braiding
(
4.4
):

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},v_{2}),v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{2}|\,|v_{3}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{3})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(-1)^{(|v_{2}|+|v_{3}|)\,|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{3})),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(v_{1}),v_{2},v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{3}\big(v_{1},\ell_{1}(v_{2}),v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{1}|+|v_{2}|}\,\ell_{3}\big(v_{1},v_{2},\ell_{1}(v_{3})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{3}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\big)\ .

(4.6)

4.2
Drinfel’d twist deformations of
L_{\infty}
-algebras

A large class of examples of braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras can be obtained from Drinfel’d twist
deformation quantization, starting from suitable
L_{\infty}
-algebras
in the classical sense. Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
in the category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules. Concretely, this
means that
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,V_{k}
