is a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded (left)

U\mathfrak{v}
-module and the
n
-brackets
\ell_{n}:\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V\to V
are
equivariant maps, that is, they all commute with the action of

\mathfrak{v}=\Gamma(TM)
on
V
via the trivial coproduct
\Delta
. Given any
Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, we can
deform the brackets
\ell_{n}
to twisted brackets
\ell_{n}^{\star}
which
are morphisms in the twisted representation category

{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules, that is, they
commute with the action of
\Gamma(TM)
on
V[[\hbar]]
via the
twisted coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
. Following the
standard prescription (
3.8
), we set

\ell_{1}^{\star}:=\ell_{1}
and

\displaystyle\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}):=\ell_{n}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})

(4.7)

for
n\geq 2
, where
v\otimes_{\star}v^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(v\otimes v^{\prime})=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v^{\prime})
for
v,v^{\prime}\in V
.

Proposition 4.8
.

If
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category

\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, then
(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\})
is a braided

L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Proof.

Braided antisymmetry of the
n
-brackets
\ell_{n}^{\star}
follows by the
definition (
4.7
), and the only thing we need to check
is that the braided homotopy relations for
\ell_{n}^{\star}
follow from
the (undeformed) homotopy relations for
\ell_{n}
. The proof follows straightforwardly from well-known abstract
arguments, see e.g.
[
16
,
17
]
, and should be obvious to experts, but we spell out the details here for the benefit of the non-expert reader.

Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization produces a
symmetric monoidal functor
\mathscr{F}:(\mathscr{M}^{\sharp},\otimes)\to{}(_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp},\otimes_{\mathcal{F}})

which is an equivalence, where
\otimes_{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the tensor product of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules acted upon using the twisted coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
. The functor
\mathscr{F}
acts on objects and morphisms as the
identity,
7
7

7

To be precise, we need to precompose
\mathscr{F}
with the
symmetric monoidal functor which sends an object
V
in

\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
to the object
V[[\hbar]]
in the symmetric monoidal
category of (left)
U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
-modules over
\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
,
see
[
16
]
for details. We suppress this extra step
here for clarity of exposition and because we will not need it in our
applications later on.
with the coherence maps

\varphi:\mathscr{F}(V)\otimes_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{F}(W)\to\mathscr{F}(V\otimes W)
given by

\displaystyle\varphi(v\otimes w)=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(w)=v\otimes_{\star}w\ ,

where
V,W
are
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules with
v\in V
and
w\in W
(the inverse is given by acting
with the twist
\mathcal{F}={\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}
). These fit into the
commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{F}(V)\otimes_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{F}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}(W)\otimes_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{F}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}(V\otimes W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathscr{F}(\tau^{\sharp})}
\textstyle{\mathscr{F}(W\otimes V)}

in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
. The coherence maps are iterated to arbitrary
n
-fold tensor products of
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules
V_{i}
to get maps
\varphi_{n}:\mathscr{F}(V_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{F}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{F}(V_{n})\to\mathscr{F}(V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{n})
for
n\geq 1
defined by

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})=v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n}\ ,

for
v_{i}\in V_{i}
, with
\varphi_{2}=\varphi
and
\varphi_{1}=\mathrm{id}
.

The morphisms (
4.7
) are thus
defined by precomposing
\mathscr{F}(\ell_{n})
with the coherence
maps
\varphi_{n}
. We will show that applying this to the morphisms (
4.2
) in

\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
gives morphisms

\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\star}=\mathscr{F}(\mathcal{J}_{n})\circ\varphi_{n}
in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, where

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{\star}_{n}:=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\,(-1)^{i\,(n-i)}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{i,n-i}}\,\text{sgn}(\sigma)\ \ell^{\star}_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell^{\star}_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma\ .

(4.13)

Since the functor
\mathscr{F}
preserves the braidings by the diagram
(
4.2
), these are just the braided homotopy
relations, i.e.
\mathcal{J}_{n}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})=0
in
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\star}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})=\mathcal{J}_{n}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})=0

in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
.

Let us now demonstrate that
\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\star}=\mathcal{J}_{n}\circ\varphi_{n}
explicitly. Note that this is trivially satisfied for
n=1
, as
\ell_{1}^{\star}=\ell_{1}
and
\varphi_{1}=\mathrm{id}
. We will show that

\displaystyle\ell^{\star}_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell^{\star}_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma=\ell_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)\circ\,\sigma\circ\varphi_{n}

for each
n\geq 2
,
i=1,\dots,n
and
\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{i,n-i}
. A simple expansion shows

\displaystyle\ell^{\star}_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell^{\star}_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{n+1-i}\circ\varphi_{n+1-i}\circ\big((\ell_{i}\circ\varphi_{i})\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i}\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{n+1-i}\circ\varphi_{n+1-i}\,\circ(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\circ(\varphi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})

\displaystyle=\ell_{n+1-i}\circ(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\circ\varphi^{i,n-i}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i}\otimes\varphi_{n-i})\circ(\varphi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\ ,

where
\varphi^{i,n-i}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}):=(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i})\otimes_{\star}(v_{i+1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{n}),
and the third equality follows from

\displaystyle\varphi_{n+1-i}\,\circ(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle=\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\big(\ell_{i}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i})\big)\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(v_{i+1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}\big(\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i})\big)\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(v_{i+1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})

\displaystyle=(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\circ\varphi^{i,n-i}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i}\otimes\varphi_{n-i})(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})\ ,

where
\ell_{i}
commutes with
\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}
since it is a morphism in
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
.
Since
v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n}=(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{i})\otimes_{\star}(v_{i+1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})
, the map
\varphi_{n}
may be equivalently written as

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{i,n-i}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i}\otimes\varphi_{n-i})\circ(\varphi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\ ,

and hence

\displaystyle\ell^{\star}_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell^{\star}_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)=\ell_{n+1-i}\circ(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\circ\varphi_{n}\ .

Next we show that
\varphi_{n}
commutes with any transposition via the braiding isomorphism
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\tau^{\sharp}
. That is, for any
n\geq 2
and
j=0,1,\dots,n-2
one has

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})=(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\circ\varphi_{n}\ .

Since
v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n}=(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{j})\otimes_{\star}\big((v_{j+1}\otimes_{\star}v_{j+2})\otimes_{\star}(v_{j+3}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})\big)
, the map
\varphi_{n}
may be also written as

\varphi_{n}=\varphi^{j,n-j}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi^{2,n-j-2})\circ(\varphi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\varphi_{n-j-2})\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\ .

Since
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\tau^{\sharp}=\varphi^{-1}\circ\tau^{\sharp}\circ\varphi
by (
4.2
), we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})

\displaystyle=\varphi^{j,n-j}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi^{2,n-j-2})\circ(\varphi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\varphi_{n-j-2})

\displaystyle\quad\,\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})

\displaystyle=\varphi^{j,n-j}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi^{2,n-j-2})\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})

\displaystyle\quad\,\circ(\varphi_{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\varphi_{n-j-2})\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\ .

The map involving the transposition commutes with the remaining maps since

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi^{2,n-j-2})\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j}\otimes\big(\Delta(\bar{{\sf f}}^{k})\circ\tau^{\sharp}\big)(v_{j+1}\otimes v_{j+2})\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(v_{j+3}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j}\otimes\big(\tau^{\sharp}\circ\Delta(\bar{{\sf f}}^{k})\big)(v_{j+1}\otimes v_{j+2})\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(v_{j+3}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\varphi^{2,n-j-2})(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})\ ,

where the second equality follows since the trivial coproduct
\Delta
is cocommutative,
\Delta^{\rm op}=\Delta
, and so
\Delta\circ\tau^{\sharp}=\tau^{\sharp}\circ\Delta^{\rm op}=\tau^{\sharp}\circ\Delta
. Similarly one has

\displaystyle\varphi^{j,n-j}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j})\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\big(\tau^{\sharp}(v_{j+1}\otimes v_{j+2})\otimes v_{j+3}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j})\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\big(\tau^{\sharp}(v_{j+1}\otimes v_{j+2})\big)\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(v_{j+3}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j})\otimes\big(\Delta(\bar{{\sf f}}_{k{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}})\circ\tau^{\sharp}\big)(v_{j+1}\otimes v_{j+2})\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(v_{j+3}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{j})\otimes\big(\tau^{\sharp}\circ\Delta(\bar{{\sf f}}_{k{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}})\big)(v_{j+1}\otimes v_{j+2})\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(v_{j+3}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\circ\varphi^{j,n-j}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})\ .

Collecting everything together, we have shown that

\displaystyle\ell^{\star}_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell^{\star}_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)\circ\,(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt=\ell_{n+1-i}\circ\big(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n-i}}\big)\circ\,(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes\tau^{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-j-2})\circ\varphi_{n}

for all
n\geq 2
,
i=1,\dots,n
and
j=0,1,\dots,n-2
. Since any permutation can be written as a composition of transpositions, the result follows immediately.
∎

Example 4.14
.

If
\ell_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 3
, then Proposition
4.8
is just the statement that a differential graded Lie algebra twist quantizes to a differential (graded) braided Lie algebra. This is precisely what we saw in Section
3.3
for the special case of exterior differential forms valued in a Lie algebra.

4.3
Cyclic structures

So far we have not discussed cyclic structures on our braided

L_{\infty}
-infinity algebras, which as we saw in
Section
2.2
is a crucial ingredient for a classical
field theory to have a Lagrangian formulation in the

L_{\infty}
-algebra
framework. Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
be a cyclic

L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded

U\mathfrak{v}
-modules. This means that the cyclic pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
has to additionally be

U\mathfrak{v}
-invariant:

\displaystyle\langle X_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}(v_{1}),X_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}(v_{2})\rangle=0

(4.15)

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
; for vector fields

\xi\in\mathfrak{v}=\Gamma(TM)
this reads

\langle\xi(v_{1}),v_{2}\rangle=-\langle v_{1},\xi(v_{2})\rangle
, which is
the natural requirement of diffeomorphism invariance of the pairing.

Following our standard prescription (
3.8
), we can twist
deform the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
to a new pairing

\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}
defined by

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\star}:=\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v_{1}),\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{2})\rangle\ .

(4.16)

Non-degeneracy of the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}:V[[\hbar]]\otimes V[[\hbar]]\to\mathbbm{R}[[\hbar]]
follows from the untwisted non-degeneracy and an order by order argument in the formal deformation parameter
\hbar
. In general, graded symmetry of the cyclic pairing
\langle-,-\rangle

implies that the twisted pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}
is naturally
(graded) braided symmetric, and the natural notion of cyclicity for a
braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra would of course be that of ‘braided
cyclicity’ defined by inserting suitable factors of the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix
in the obvious way. However, this level of generality is not
suitable for applications to field theory, as the loss of
strict
graded
symmetry and cyclicity would lead to problems with the variational principle for the corresponding action functionals. We therefore
restrict from the outset the types of Drinfel’d twists that we will
use for deformation quantization of a given cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Definition 4.17
.

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
is

compatible
with a cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
on an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
if

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{2})\rangle_{\star}=\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\star}

for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
.

Given the concrete examples of cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra structures
that we considered in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
,
it should come as no surprise that this definition is an abstraction
of the graded cyclicity property (
3.10
) for integration
of twisted differential forms which we already discussed in
Section
3.3
, and with it we have

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
be a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
in the category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a compatible Drinfel’d
twist. Then

(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\star})
is a strictly
cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Proof.

For clarity of exposition, in the following we drop the signs arising
from the gradings in the cyclicity conditions of
Section
2.1
, as they play no role in the proof since they are the same for the original and the twist deformed
L_{\infty}
-algebra. We then need to show
that

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\rangle_{\star}=\langle v_{n},\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1})\rangle_{\star}\ .

For
n=1
, since
\ell_{1}^{\star}=\ell_{1}
is not deformed, the left-hand side reads

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},\ell_{1}(v_{1})\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(v_{0}),\ell_{1}(\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(v_{1}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(v_{1}),\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\ell_{1}(v_{0}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{1}),\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\ell_{1}(v_{0}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{1}),\mathsf{R}^{l}(\ell_{1}(v_{0}))\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{1},\ell_{1}(v_{0})\rangle_{\star}

where in the first and fourth equalities we use the definition of the
star-pairing, in the first and second equalities we use commutativity
of
\ell_{1}
with the
U\mathfrak{v}
-action, in the second equality we use
classical cyclicity, in the third equality the definition of the

{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix, and in the fifth equality the compatibility of the
twist.

For
n\geq 2
, this calculation extends to

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},\ell_{n}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v_{0}),\bar{\sf f}_{k}\big(\ell_{n}(\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{n}))\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v_{0}),\ell_{n}\big(\bar{\sf f}_{k}{}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}{}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{n})\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0}),\ell_{n}\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{n})\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{n}),\ell_{n}\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}^{k}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{n}),\bar{\sf f}^{k}\big(\ell_{n}(\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1}))\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{m}(v_{n}),\bar{\sf f}_{k}\mathsf{R}^{m}\big(\ell_{n}(\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1}))\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{m}(v_{n}),\mathsf{R}^{m}\big(\ell_{n}(v_{0}\otimes_{\star}v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\big)\big\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{n},\ell_{n}(v_{0}\otimes_{\star}v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\rangle_{\star}

where we used the same steps as in the
n=1
case above, and now also
the cocycle property (
3.1
) for the inverse of the
Drinfel’d twist and the
U\mathfrak{v}
-invariance (
4.15
) of
the classical pairing in the third equality.
∎

5
Braided field theory

5.1
Noncommutative field theories in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

We can now build up large classes of noncommutative field theories by
employing a braided version of the ‘bottoms-up’ approach to classical
field theories which we discussed in
Section
2.2
, whereby a classical field theory is defined as a dynamical system by its
4-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra as the initial input. These can be constructed starting
from any suitable braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the sense of
Section
4.1
; we call a field theory obtained in this
way a
braided field theory
. That is, in this paper, a braided field theory is
defined as a (formal) dynamical system by its 4-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra as initial input.
8
8

8

Our formalism may however also be used to treat more general field theories with reducible gauge symmetries, which are captured by
k
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras with
k>4
.

Usually these theories will come from
deforming a classical field theory on a manifold
M
through Drinfel’d
twist deformation quantization of
its corresponding
L_{\infty}
-algebra, as described in
Section
4.2
, and indeed this will be the case for
the examples treated in this paper. If
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is a 4-term

L_{\infty}
-algebra with underlying graded vector space

\displaystyle V=V_{0}\oplus V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\oplus V_{3}

in the representation category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, then, following the
prescription of Section
4.2
, we construct the
corresponding 4-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\})
in the twisted representation category

{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
.
In simple
instances, a braided field theory obtained in this way through
Drinfel’d twist deformation of a classical Lagrangian field theory will be the
“naive” deformation, obtained by twisting products of fields in the equations of motion and in
the action functional to star-products in the standard way. However,
we shall see that this is not always the case, a notable example being
the four-dimensional braided ECP theory of gravity that we define
later on. But even in simple cases the notion of braided gauge
symmetry brings with it several novelties and complexities that we
explain in detail below.

We shall now run through the various ingredients
involved in the definition of a noncommutative field theory in this
way for a general 4-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
, following the
standard classical prescription of Section
2.2
.

5.2
Braided Lie algebras of gauge symmetries

Let us first discuss what should generally be meant by a ‘gauge symmetry’ in this setting, and then later pass to its realization in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism.

Braided gauge transformations

In the context of generalized gauge symmetries, wherein one considers general non-linear field-dependent transformations, a
braided gauge transformation
is a map
V_{0}\times V_{1}\to V_{1}
, denoted as
(\lambda,A)\mapsto\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A
, which is linear in the gauge parameters
\lambda\in V_{0}
and has a potentially arbitrary polynomial dependence on fields
A\in V_{1}
. This means that the map may be realized as the diagonal of a map
V_{0}\otimes\text{\Large$\odot$}^{\bullet}V_{1}\to V_{1}
as an arrow in the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, where the symmetric tensor product is taken with respect to the braided transposition
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}
. Explicitly, a braided gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A
may be expanded in tensor powers of the fields
A^{\otimes n}
as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A=\delta_{0}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}(\lambda)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\delta_{\lambda,n}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}(A^{\otimes n})\ ,

where each polynomial order is now a linear braided symmetric map on
V_{1}
in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
. Similarly to the classical case, the collection of transformations on fields
V_{1}
are required to close (in a suitable sense to be made precise later on) under the braided commutator bracket

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}:=\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\circ\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}-\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\circ\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\ ,

for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in V_{0}
.
The braided commutator is a braided Lie bracket, that is, it is braided antisymmetric and satisfies the braided Jacobi identity; this is a special instance of the braided commutator of endomorphisms in the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
which makes the braided derivations of a
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra into a braided Lie algebra
[
17
]
. The simplest prototypical case, and in fact the case most relevant for the examples of this paper, is that of a linear braided Lie algebra action, which we discuss in Example
5.6
below.

By setting
\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}\big|_{V_{0}}=0
, a braided gauge transformation is extended to a map
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}:V\to V
of degree
0
as a braided derivation of operations defined on
V\otimes V
.
It is convenient to formalise the braided derivation property by stating that the action of a braided gauge transformation
\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}
on a
tensor product is through the twisted coproduct

\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda})=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathrm{id}+\mathsf{R}_{k}\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}\ .

This is also a map
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}):V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
of degree
0
, which encodes the braided Leibniz rule. This map is extended inductively to higher tensor products
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}):\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V\to\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V
for
n\geq 3
by

\displaystyle\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda})=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k_{i}}\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k_{i}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(\lambda)}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i-1}\ ,

(5.1)

and we set
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}(\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}):=\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}}
and
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}):=\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}})
.
Thus if
\mu_{n}
is any map on
\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V
in the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, we define its braided gauge transformation by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\mu_{n}:=\mu_{n}\circ\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}})\ .

(5.2)

For example, with
n=2
one has

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\mu_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=\mu_{2}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}v_{1}\otimes v_{2})+\mu_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2}\big)

(5.3)

for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
.

The superscript
{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}
is used to indicate that this map is regarded as a
left
braided gauge transformation which “acts from the left”. Correspondingly, there is a
right
braided gauge transformation
V_{1}\times V_{0}\to V_{1}
,
(A,\lambda)\mapsto\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}A
which is regarded as “acting from the right” and is defined by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}A:=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)=\delta_{0}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}(\lambda)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda),n}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes n})\big)\ .

In the classical case, where
\mathcal{R}=1\otimes 1
, the two transformations of course coincide.
The action of a right braided gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}
on tensor products is through the twisted coproduct

\displaystyle\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny R}}_{\lambda})=\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny R}}_{\lambda}+\delta^{\textrm{\tiny R}}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}\ ,

encoding the braided Leibniz rule from the right, and its higher iterations

\displaystyle\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny R}}_{\lambda})=\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-1}\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny R}}_{\lambda}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i-1}\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny R}}_{\mathsf{R}_{k_{i}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}(\lambda)}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{i}}\ .

(5.4)

Suppose now that the map
\mu_{2}
is braided symmetric, that is, the maps

\displaystyle\mu_{2}=\mu_{2}\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}

are equal as morphisms in the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, where
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}:V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
is the braiding given by
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}:={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\circ\tau^{\sharp}=\tau^{\sharp}\circ{\mathcal{R}}
with
\tau^{\sharp}
the graded transposition. On homogeneous elements this reads

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=(-1)^{|v_{1}|\,|v_{2}|}\,\mu_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{1})\big)\ .

(5.5)

We check that the definition (
5.2
) is compatible with this braided symmetry, that is, that the left braided gauge transformation of the right-hand side of (
5.5
) is equal to (
5.3
):

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\big(\mu_{2}\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}\big)(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})

\displaystyle:=\big(\mu_{2}\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\circ\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}\circ\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}})\big)(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|v_{1}|\,|v_{2}|}\,\big(\mu_{2}\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\big)\big(v_{2}\otimes\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}v_{1}+\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|v_{1}|\,|v_{2}|}\,\mu_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{l}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}v_{1})+\mathsf{R}_{l}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})\big)

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}\big(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}v_{1}\otimes v_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mu_{2}\circ\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda})\big)(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})

\displaystyle=:\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\mu_{2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})\ ,

where in the fourth equality we used braided symmetry (
5.5
).
This consistency check also obviously holds when
\mu_{2}
is braided antisymmetric, and it easily extends to braided (anti)symmetric maps
\mu_{n}
on higher tensor products
\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V
for
n\geq 3
. Similarly, one verifies consistency with right braided gauge transformations.

Note that here and in the following the ordering between the braiding isomorphism and gauge transformations is important: one must write a map as a composition of morphisms in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
and precompose them with
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}},{\textrm{\tiny R}}})
as in the definition (
5.2
), including
\mu_{2}\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}
, because in general the braiding and the twisted coproduct do not commute:
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}\circ\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}},{\textrm{\tiny R}}})\neq\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}},{\textrm{\tiny R}}})\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}^{\phantom{{\dagger}}}\tau^{\sharp}_{V,V}
. In other words

\displaystyle\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny L}}})\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{l}(v_{1})\big)\neq\mathsf{R}_{l}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})+\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{2})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{l}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}v_{1})\ ,

and similarly for right gauge transformations.

Drinfel’d twist deformations of gauge symmetries

If our noncommutative field theory arises from Drinfel’d twist quantization of a classical field theory on a manifold
M
, then a left braided gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}:V[[\hbar]]\to V[[\hbar]]
is induced from a classical gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}:V\to V
through the usual prescription (
3.8
):

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}v:=\delta_{\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\lambda)}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v)

for
\lambda\in V_{0}
and
v\in V
. The corresponding right braided gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}:V[[\hbar]]\to V[[\hbar]]
is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}v=\delta^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}\mathsf{R}^{k}(v)=\delta_{\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\lambda)}\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v)\ ,

where in the second equality we used
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{21}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{21}
.

Example 5.6
.

Let us look at the prototypical case that will
apply to all examples in this paper. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra with Lie bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
let
W
be a linear representation of
\mathfrak{g}
; we denote the action of
\mathfrak{g}

on
W
by
\triangleright:\mathfrak{g}\otimes W\to W
. The action of a gauge
parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
on a
p
-form field

\phi\in\Omega^{p}(M,W)
valued in
W
is given by
9
9

9

The minus sign is the standard convention in gauge theory, so that the action of the corresponding Lie group is a right action.

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\phi=-\lambda\triangleright\phi\ .

(5.7)

In this definition we make no distinction between left and right
actions.

To pass to the twist deformed field theory, we regard the
representation spaces
W
as trivial
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules and

\Omega^{p}(M,W)
as the tensor product of
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules, similarly to
the graded Lie algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})

(cf. Section
3.3
). We may then define both a
left braided representation

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi=-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\phi:=-\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\lambda)\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\phi)

(5.8)

and a right braided representation

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\phi=-\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda:=-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)=-\bar{\sf f}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{l}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)=-\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\lambda)\triangleright\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\phi)\ ,

(5.9)

where the first expression encodes the intuition of swapping factors
and then acting on the representation. The twisted coproducts
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}})
and
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}})
for
n\geq 2
then define the tensor products of left and right braided representations, respectively.

Both left and right gauge transformations provide a representation of the braided Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)
on
\Omega^{p}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
:

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}},{\textrm{\tiny R}}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}},{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}},{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\ ,

(5.10)

for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. For this, we compute

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\big(-\lambda_{2}\triangleright_{\star}\phi\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright_{\star}\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}_{m{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(\phi)\big)

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}{\sf f}^{p}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}{\sf f}_{p}\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)

where in the fourth line we used the cocycle property (
3.1
) for the inverse of the Drinfel’d twist, in the fifth line we used
{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}\,\mathcal{F}_{21}^{-1}
, and in the last step we used
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,\mathcal{F}=1\otimes 1
along with cocommutativity of the trivial coproduct
\Delta
. With analogous calculations, this correspondingly gives

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2})}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)=\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)\ .

Thus we get

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}\phi

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)-\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(\lambda_{1})\triangleright\big(\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(\lambda_{2})\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)\big)

\displaystyle=-[\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\bar{\sf f}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),\bar{\sf f}^{m}_{{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\bar{\sf f}_{l}(\lambda_{2})]_{\mathfrak{g}}\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\bar{\sf f}^{m}\big(-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\triangleright\bar{\sf f}_{m}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\delta_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi\ ,

where in the second line we used the fact that (
5.7
) is a representation of
\mathfrak{g}
on
\Omega^{p}(M,W)
. The proof for right gauge transformations is completely analogous.

For the special case when the field
\phi
is a gauge connection
a\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
,
W
is the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
, and the left and right braided adjoint actions are given by

\displaystyle\lambda\triangleright_{\star}^{\rm ad}a:=[\lambda,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad a\,^{\rm ad}\!\!{}_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda:=[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda),\mathsf{R}^{k}(a)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=-[a,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

where in the last equality we used braided antisymmetry of the braided Lie bracket involving gauge parameters. For a matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
one can write

\displaystyle[\lambda,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=\lambda\star a-\mathsf{R}_{k}(a)\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)\ ,

where the star-product here is the tensor product of matrix multiplication with the twisted product of functions and forms.
The left and right braided adjoint representations induce the left and right braided gauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}a:=\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}a:=\mathrm{d}\lambda+[a,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

These give the representations (
5.10
) on
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
. This can be shown by using braided antisymmetry and the braided Jacobi identity for the bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
, along with the undeformed Leibniz rule for the exterior derivative
\mathrm{d}
(cf. Section
3.3
); it will follow as a special case of a more general result in Section
5.5
below (see Example
5.24
).

The braiding of symmetries illustrated by Example
5.6
is independent of the particular form of a (classical) Lie algebra
V_{0}
of gauge parameters: the only necessary condition is that
V_{0}
is a Lie algebra in the category of
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules.

5.3
Braided versus star-gauge symmetry

Example
5.6
illustrates an important distinction between braided gauge symmetries and the well-known star-gauge symmetries. These latter gauge transformations are the most prominent in the noncommutative field theory literature, with applications ranging from the Standard Model to gravity, and they are the ones which are consistent with the embeddings of these theories into string theory. In these more conventional settings, one works with a matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and introduces a star-commutator bracket

\displaystyle[\lambda_{1}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\!\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\lambda_{1}\star\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{2}\star\lambda_{1}\ ,

(5.11)

where here the product is the tensor product of star-multiplication with matrix multiplication of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. Demanding closure as an ordinary (not braided) Lie algebra is then not possible for gauge algebras beyond the unitary Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{u}(N)
in the fundamental representation, unless one extends the space of gauge parameters
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})

[
4
]
. This correspondingly introduces new dynamical degrees of freedom into the noncommutative field theory, which are hard to justify or motivate from a physical standpoint, and moreover cause problems when considering commutative limits of the theories. One can avoid the introduction of new fields by instead regarding (
5.11
) as an element of the enveloping algebra
U\mathfrak{g}[[\hbar]]
and using the Seiberg–Witten map
[
38
]
; however, the Seiberg–Witten map is not generally known in closed form and so this approach is limited to a description of star-gauge transformations to only the first few orders in the deformation parameter
\hbar
.

In contrast, in the present setting, the Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)
of gauge parameters is deformed to the braided Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)
without the need of extending the vector space
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
(beyond the usual formal power series extension), for
arbitrary
Lie algebras. Even for a matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, using
\mathcal{F}_{21}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{21}
one finds that the braided commutator

\displaystyle[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=\lambda_{1}\star\lambda_{2}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})

(5.12)

is not equal to the star-commutator (
5.11
), even for the unitary Lie algebras and for simple twists like the Moyal–Weyl star-product of Example
3.9
; a striking example is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{u}(1)
(or any abelian Lie algebra), where the braided commutator (
5.12
) always vanishes but the star-commutator (
5.11
) does not.
In our approach the twisting of the gauge algebra and its representations themselves avoids the introduction of new fields altogether. The dynamical sector of the noncommutative field theory is then correspondingly constructed by twist deforming the entire underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure.

A hybrid definition of ‘twisted gauge symmetry’, which in a sense lies in between the standard star-gauge transformations and our braided gauge transformations, was suggested in
[
46
,
53
,
14
]
. In our setting, this would follow from twist deforming the coproduct of gauge transformations
\triangle(\delta_{\lambda})
by regarding the twist
\mathcal{F}
in the universal enveloping algebra of the semi-direct product Lie algebra
\Gamma(TM)\ltimes\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. Then the gauge transformations are just the usual classical gauge transformations, but they act as braided derivations similarly to our braided gauge transformations. Since the actions of
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
themselves are not deformed, the closure of gauge transformations is the same as in the classical case: the gauge algebra closes in the classical Lie algebra for all Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
and their representations. However, this approach inevitably also involves star-commutators, rather than braided commutators, and so also introduces additional dynamical degrees of freedom
[
14
]
, since the star-commutator does not generally close in the (matrix) Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. This mixing between braided and star-gauge transformations also does not appear to be consistent or natural within the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
.

5.4
Braided noncommutative kinematics

Continuing Example
5.6
, we will now define the natural geometric objects which are covariant under braided gauge transformations. These will form the building blocks for the braided gauge-invariant noncommutative field theories that we study in detail later on. Aspects of this story have been studied extensively at the kinematical level by
[
23
]
, and also alluded to by
[
49
]
. We will see how this extends to include dynamics by systematically defining the full theory using the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism, which constructs braided field theories in a model-independent way.

Braided covariant derivatives

Given a (classical) connection
1
-form
a\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, the covariant derivative
\mathrm{d}^{a}\phi\in\Omega^{p+1}(M,W)
of any field
\phi\in\Omega^{p}(M,W)
is given classically by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{a}\phi=\mathrm{d}\phi+a\triangleright\phi\ ,

where the action of
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
on
\Omega^{p}(M,W)
is the tensor product of exterior multiplication of forms with the action of
\mathfrak{g}
on the representation
W
. In the twist deformed field theory, the transformation of
\phi
under left gauge transformations (
5.8
) suggests that the correct definition of the
left
covariant derivative is simply by acting with the corresponding left braided representation:

Definition 5.13
.

Given a (braided) connection 1-form
a\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, the
left braided covariant derivative
of a field
\phi\in\Omega^{p}(M,W)
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{a}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi:=\mathrm{d}\phi+a\triangleright_{\star}\phi\ .

This is indeed covariant under left braided gauge transformations:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}\phi)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}(\mathrm{d}\phi+a\triangleright_{\star}\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi+\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}a\triangleright_{\star}\phi+\mathsf{R}_{k}(a)\triangleright_{\star}\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\phi

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}(\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\phi)+(\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\triangleright_{\star}\phi-\mathsf{R}_{k}(a)\triangleright_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\phi\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\phi-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\mathrm{d}\phi+\mathrm{d}\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\phi-[\lambda,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\triangleright_{\star}\phi-\mathsf{R}_{k}(a)\triangleright_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\phi\big)

\displaystyle=-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\mathrm{d}\phi-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}(a\triangleright_{\star}\phi)

\displaystyle=-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}\phi)\ ,

where in the second and fifth lines we used the twisted coproduct
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}})
to implement the Leibniz rule from the left.

Similarly, we can show that the left covariant derivative is also covariant under
right
braided gauge transformations (
5.9
):

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}\phi)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}(\mathrm{d}\phi+a\triangleright_{\star}\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\phi+a\triangleright_{\star}\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\phi+\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}a\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(-\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda)+a\triangleright_{\star}(-\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda)+\big(\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)+[a,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda-\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\mathrm{d}\lambda-a\triangleright_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)\big)+\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\mathrm{d}\lambda+[a,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda-[a,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)-\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{l}(a)\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)\big)+[a,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(a\triangleright_{\star}\phi)

\displaystyle=-(\mathrm{d}\phi+a\triangleright_{\star}\phi)\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda

\displaystyle=-(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}\phi)\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda\ ,

where in the second line we used the twisted coproduct
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}})
to implement the braided Leibniz rule from the right, in the fifth line we used the twisted coproduct
\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}})
, and in the sixth line we used the second
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity of (
3.2
).

As one might have anticipated, we can define analogously the
right
covariant derivative of
\phi\in\Omega^{p}(M,W)
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{a}\phi:=\mathrm{d}\phi+(-1)^{p}\,\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft a=\mathrm{d}\phi+\mathsf{R}_{k}(a)\triangleright_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)\ .

Similar calculations to those of the left covariant derivative establish that the right covariant derivative is covariant under both left and right braided gauge transformations.

What distinguishes the left and right braided covariant derivatives is their behaviour with respect to the action of the noncommutative algebra of functions on
M
. The vector space of braided fields
\Omega^{p}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
carrying a braided representation of
\mathfrak{g}
is a bimodule over the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\star}=\big(C^{\infty}(M)[[\hbar]],\star\big)
: one can star-multiply with functions on the left and on the right to produce
p
-forms which transform identically under left and right braided gauge transformations, because functions transform in the trivial representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. For example, for a function
f\in\mathcal{A}_{\star}
we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}(f\star\phi)=\mathsf{R}_{k}(f)\star\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}\phi=\mathsf{R}_{k}(f)\star\big(-\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)\triangleright_{\star}\phi\big)=-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}(f\star\phi)\ ,

where in the last equality we used the left braided Leibniz rule with
\lambda
acting via the trivial representation on
f
. Similar results hold for right star-multiplication and right gauge transformations.

However, the left and right covariant derivatives do not satisfy the expected (undeformed) Leibniz rules when viewing
\Omega^{p}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
as a bimodule over
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
, but only as right and left
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
-modules respectively:
\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}
is a derivation with respect to right star-multiplication by
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
and analogously
\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{a}
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
-module map. This is where potential confusion for our left/right definitions may arise. Explicitly, it is easy to check the derivation properties

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}(\phi\star f)=\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{a}\phi\star f+(-1)^{p}\,\phi\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}f\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{a}(f\star\phi)=\mathrm{d}f\wedge_{\star}\phi+f\star\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{a}\phi\ ,

but the Leibniz rule does not hold for the other two
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
-module actions (here
\wedge_{\star}
is simply the twisted exterior product of the form parts).

In classical differential geometry one can define a connection directly as the covariant derivative map. In noncommutative differential geometry, one has two options which are
a priori
different: a left module connection and a right module connection (see e.g.
[
49
,
3
]
). In contrast, here we have a single gauge connection
a
which induces both covariant derivatives.

Braided curvature

The left curvature of the gauge field
a
is now similarly defined with
W
the adjoint representation and the usual factor of
\frac{1}{2}
:

Definition 5.14
.

Given a braided gauge field
a\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, the
left braided curvature
is

\displaystyle F_{a}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}:=\mathrm{d}a+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[a,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(5.15)

The right curvature is correspondingly

\displaystyle F_{a}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}:=\mathrm{d}a+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(a),\mathsf{R}^{k}(a)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}a+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[a,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=F_{a}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\ ,

where we used braided symmetry of the bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
on
1
-forms. Hence there is only one curvature
F_{a}^{\star}:=F_{a}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}=F_{a}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}
. The proof of left and right covariance of the curvature follows from analogous calculations to those above:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{a}=-[\lambda,F^{\star}_{a}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}F^{\star}_{a}=[F^{\star}_{a},\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

For a matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, one can write

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{a}=\mathrm{d}a+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(a\wedge_{\star}a+\mathsf{R}_{k}(a)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(a)\big)\ ,

where here the star-product is the tensor product of matrix multiplication with the twisted exterior product; this differs from the usual definition in conventional noncommutative gauge theories, where one would instead write
F_{a}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}a+a\wedge_{\star}a
.

One of the novelties of the braided curvature
2
-form is that it generally violates the Bianchi identity, that is, it is no longer covariantly constant. The braided deformations of the Bianchi identity are the three-forms in
\Omega^{3}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{a}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F_{a}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,[F_{a},a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[a,F_{a}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(a),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(a),a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{a}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}F_{a}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[F_{a},a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[a,F_{a}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(a),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(a),a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(5.16)

Both of these relations simply follow by taking the exterior derivative of the expression (
5.15
) and rewriting it in terms of
F_{a}
to generate the differential identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}F_{a}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,[F_{a},a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[a,F_{a}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big([[a,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star},a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-[a,[a,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ ,

and using the (odd) braided Jacobi identity (
3.11
) on
1
-forms
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
with
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=a
; contrary to the classical case, this differential identity also involves the gauge field
a
itself. This violation is well-known in twisted noncommutative differential geometry, see e.g.
[
18
,
11
,
3
]
. For trivial
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix, the right-hand sides of these covariant derivative equations vanish identically and we recover the
usual Bianchi identity in classical geometry.

One of the remarkable features of our braided field theory formalism will be that such kinematical violations are simply natural consequences of the braided homotopy relations for the underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra, as we discuss further later on. In other words, the violations of the Bianchi identity here will be controlled by a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra. Let us finally turn to this formalism.

5.5
Braided gauge transformations and
L_{\infty}
-algebras

Generalizing Example
5.6
, let us now formulate a braided version of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
gauge transformations (
2.4
). Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
be a
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of graded left
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules. Following the classical case, we have

Definition 5.17
.

For a gauge parameter
\lambda\in V_{0}
, the
left braided gauge transformation
of a field
A\in V_{1}
is

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A:=\ell_{1}(\lambda)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n+1}(\lambda,A,\dots,A)\ .

(5.18)

The right braided gauge transformation is similarly defined by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}A:=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(\lambda)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A\otimes\dots\otimes A)\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(\lambda)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n+1)}}\,\ell_{n+1}(A,\dots,A,\lambda)\ ,

(5.19)

where in the last equality we used braided antisymmetry of the
n{+}1
-brackets under interchange of gauge parameters and fields, together with the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities in (
3.2
) (and their inverses) to write

\displaystyle\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A\otimes\cdots\otimes A)\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n}}(\lambda),\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(A),\dots,\mathsf{R}^{k_{n}}(A)\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l_{1}}\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(A),\dots,\mathsf{R}_{l_{n}}\mathsf{R}^{k_{n}}(A),\mathsf{R}^{l_{n}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{l_{1}}\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n}}(\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(A\otimes\cdots\otimes A),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,\ell_{n+1}(A,\dots,A,\lambda)\ .

(5.20)

Braided Lie algebras of gauge transformations

Whereas a closure statement for the braided gauge
L_{\infty}
-algebra should follow as in the classical case (
2.7
), the general proof is complicated by the complex explicit forms of the braided homotopy relations. For the field theories that we study in this paper, the gauge algebra is always field-independent and closes off-shell, that is, without the need to impose any field equations. In these instances, it is relatively straightforward to establish closure of these left and right braided gauge transformations. For this, as suggested from the theories we shall study, we require that the
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
has the vanishing sets of brackets

\displaystyle\ell_{n+2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},A_{1},\dots,A_{n})=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{n+2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},F,A_{1},\dots,A_{n-1})=0

(5.21)

for all
n\geq 1
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in V_{0}
,
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in V_{1}
and
F\in V_{2}
. If our
L_{\infty}
-algebra is further equipped with a (braided or strictly) cyclic structure of degree
-3
, as will be the case for all field theories considered in this paper, then the two vanishing conditions in (
5.21
) are equivalent.
In this case both left and right braided gauge transformations (
5.18
) and (
5.5
) on fields
V_{1}
close the braided Lie algebra

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\textrm{\tiny L,R}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\textrm{\tiny L,R}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta_{-\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\textrm{\tiny L,R}}\ .

(5.22)

Let us indicate why the braided commutation relations (
5.22
) hold. Using (
5.18
) and the braided Leibniz rule (
5.1
) we compute the composition of two left braided gauge transformations evaluated on a field
A\in V_{1}
to get

\displaystyle\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda_{1}}\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda_{2}}A

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}}{n!}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\triangle^{n}_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})}^{\textrm{\tiny L}})(A\otimes\cdots\otimes A)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}}{n!}\,\Big(\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-1}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}(A),\dots,\mathsf{R}_{k_{i}}(A),\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k_{i}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})}A,A^{\otimes n-i-1}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m-1)}}{(n+1)!\,m!}\,\Big(\ell_{n+2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\ell_{n+2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}(A),\dots,\mathsf{R}_{k_{i}}(A),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k_{i}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m-1)}}{(n+1)!\,m!}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,\ell_{n+2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)

where in the last step we used the first identity from (
3.2
). Reordering the double sum we obtain

\displaystyle\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda_{1}}\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda_{2}}A=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}}{n!}\ \sum_{j=0}^{n}\,

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{(j-1)\,(n-j)}}{n-j+1}\,\binom{n}{j}

\displaystyle\times\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-j}\,\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)\ .

For the corresponding composition
\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}_{p}(\lambda_{2})}\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{p}(\lambda_{1})}A
, we use

\displaystyle\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}^{p}(\lambda_{1}),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}_{p}(\lambda_{2}),A^{\otimes i}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt=\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{2}),A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)\ .

Collecting terms order by order in tensor powers
A^{\otimes n}
, we see that the closure formula (
5.22
) for left gauge transformations evaluated on a field
A\in V_{1}
is satisfied if

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-j}\,\frac{(-1)^{(j-1)\,(n-j)}}{n-j+1}\,\binom{n}{j}\,\Big(

\displaystyle\,\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{2}),A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)

\displaystyle-\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\ell_{n+1}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),A^{\otimes n}\big)

(5.23)

for each
n\geq 0
. This is a consequence of the braided homotopy relations
\mathcal{J}_{n+2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},A^{\otimes n})=0
from (
4.2
) and the imposed restrictions (
5.21
). To illustrate the complicated
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix gymnastics that are involved in the proof, even with our simplifying assumptions (
5.21
), we demonstrate this explicitly for the first two orders.

For
n=0
the left-hand side of (
5.5
) evaluates to

\displaystyle\ell_{2}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2})\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}))\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{2}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2})\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{l}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}))\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{2}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2})\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\lambda_{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\big)

as required, where in the first equality we used
U\mathfrak{v}
-equivariance of the map
\ell_{1}
, in the second equality we used braided antisymmetry of the
2
-bracket
\ell_{2}
, and in the last step we used the derivation property of the homotopy identities (
4.5
).

For
n=1
the left-hand side of (
5.5
) is

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}),A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{2}))\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{2}(\lambda_{2},A)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})),A\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1})\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),A)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}),A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}))\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{l}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1})),A\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}))\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\,+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\lambda_{2},A\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}))\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda_{1}),\mathsf{R}_{m}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{m}\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\lambda_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}),A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\lambda_{2},A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{m}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1})),\mathsf{R}^{m}\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\,-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{m}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{m}(\lambda_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}),A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\lambda_{2},A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1})),\mathsf{R}^{l}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{l}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(A)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\,-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{m}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{m}(\lambda_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\lambda_{1},\ell_{1}(\lambda_{2}),A\big)-\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\lambda_{2},A\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad\,-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\mathsf{R}_{m}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{m}(\lambda_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),A\big)

as required. In the first equality we used
U\mathfrak{v}
-equivariance and braided antisymmetry of
\ell_{2}
. In the second equality we used braided antisymmetry of
\ell_{3}
and the second
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity (
3.2
). In the third equality we used braided symmetry on degree 1 elements followed by braided antisymmetry involving degree 0 elements. In the fourth equality we used the first
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity from (
3.2
).
In the final step we used the braided homotopy relation (
4.6
) together with the vanishing brackets
\ell_{3}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},A)=0
and
\ell_{3}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ell_{1}(A))=0
by our requirements (
5.21
). Similarly, one checks explicitly the leading orders of the closure relation (
5.22
) evaluated on a field
A\in V_{1}
for right braided gauge transformations (
5.5
). A complete proof, including the general statement of the closure relation, is beyond the scope of this paper.

Example 5.24
.

Returning to Example
5.6
,
let us now consider a
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra whose kinematical sector is given by the homogeneous subspaces

\displaystyle V_{0}=\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad V_{1}=\Omega^{p}(M,W)\times\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ .

Comparing the left and right braided gauge transformations with the prescriptions (
5.18
) and (
5.5
) we read off the differential as

\displaystyle\ell_{1}^{\star}(\lambda)=(0,\mathrm{d}\lambda)\ ,

while the only non-zero brackets involving gauge parameters and fields are given by

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\lambda,(\phi,a)\big)=\big(-\lambda\triangleright_{\star}\phi,-[\lambda,a]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\phi,a),\lambda\big)=\big(\phi\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\lambda,-[a,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ .

The brackets in this sector satisfy (
5.21
), and comparing (
5.10
) with (
5.22
) we find the non-zero
2
-bracket among gauge parameters

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

Example
5.24
illustrates the typical features of braided gauge symmetries in most field theories of interest in physics, and in particular those treated in the present paper. Given the complexity of the calculations illustrated above using braided homotopy relations even at the lowest orders, for simplicity and ease of exposition, in the following we will impose the further vanishing conditions

\displaystyle\ell_{n+1}(\lambda,A_{1},\dots,A_{n})=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{n+1}(\lambda,F,A_{1},\dots,A_{n-1})=0\ ,

(5.25)

for all
n\geq 2
,
\lambda\in V_{0}
,
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in V_{1}
and
F\in V_{2}
.
The first condition implies that the braided gauge transformations are at most linear in the field
A
, that is,
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A=\ell_{1}(\lambda)+\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)
and
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}A=\ell_{1}(\lambda)-\ell_{2}(A,\lambda)
. The second condition is again equivalent to the first for (braided or strictly) cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras. With these assumptions, the lowest orders calculations presented above constitute a complete proof of the closure condition (
5.22
).

5.6
Braided covariant dynamics

For a field
A\in V_{1}
, the braided analog of (
2.5
) which dictates the field equations follows exactly as in the classical case.

Definition 5.26
.

For
A\in V_{1}
, the
braided field equations

F_{A}=0
in
V_{2}
are given by

\displaystyle F_{A}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A)\ .

(5.27)

Left gauge covariance

The expression (
5.27
) is covariant under the left gauge transformations discussed in Section
5.5
, namely

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}F_{A}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,(-1)^{i}\,\ell_{n+2}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes i},F_{A},A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\ ,

(5.28)

for all gauge parameters
\lambda\in V_{0}
. In the classical case, where
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
, the expression (
5.28
) agrees with (
2.6
).

Let us indicate why (
5.28
) is true. We compute the gauge transformation of (
5.27
) using (
5.18
) and similar calculations to those in Section
5.5
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}F_{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\ell_{n}\big(\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda})(A\otimes\dots\otimes A)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m-1)}}{(n+1)!\,m!}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)

(5.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}}{n!}\,\sum_{j=0}^{n}\,\sum_{i=0}^{n-j}\,\frac{(-1)^{(j-1)\,(n-j)}}{n-j+1}\,\binom{n}{j}\,\ell_{n-j+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)

where in the last step we reorganized the double sum. On the other hand, using (
5.27
) the right-hand side of (
5.28
) is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m-1)}}{(n+1)!\,m!}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,(-1)^{i}\,\ell_{n+2}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes i},\ell_{m}(A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}}{n!}\ \sum_{j=0}^{n}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-j}\,\frac{(-1)^{j\,(n-j)+i}}{n-j+1}\,\binom{n}{j}\,\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes i},\ell_{j}(A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)

where we set
\ell_{0}:=0
.

Thus, collecting terms order by order in tensor powers of the fields
A^{\otimes n}
, we see that (
5.28
) is satisfied if

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-j}\,\frac{(-1)^{j\,(n-j)}}{n-j+1}\,\binom{n}{j}\,\Big((-1)^{n-j}\,

\displaystyle\,\ell_{n-j+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)

(5.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-(-1)^{i}\,\ell_{n-j+2}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes i},\ell_{j}(A^{\otimes j}),A^{\otimes n-i-j}\big)\Big)=0

for each
n\geq 0
. This now follows by the braided homotopy relations
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}(\lambda,A^{\otimes n})=0
from (
4.2
).
For
n=0
the expression (
5.30
) reads
\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}(\lambda))=0
, which is the differential identity in (
4.5
). For
n=1
the left-hand side of the expression (
5.30
) reads

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda),A\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda))\big)+\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\lambda,\ell_{1}(A)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt=\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\lambda,\ell_{1}(A)\big)=0

as required, where we used
U\mathfrak{v}
-equivariance of
\ell_{1}
and braided symmetry of
\ell_{2}
on degree
1
elements in the first step, and the derivation identity of (
4.5
) in the last step. For
n=2
, with similar manipulations one can reduce the left-hand side of (
5.30
) to

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{3}\,\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda),A,A\big)+\tfrac{1}{3}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)),A\big)+\tfrac{1}{3}\,\ell_{3}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda))\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,A),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A)\big)+\ell_{3}\big(\lambda,\ell_{1}(A),A)-\ell_{3}\big(\lambda,A,\ell_{1}(A)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{3}(\lambda,A,A)\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\lambda,\ell_{2}(A,A)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda),A,A\big)+\ell_{3}\big(\lambda,\ell_{1}(A),A\big)-\ell_{3}\big(A,A,\ell_{1}(A)\big)+\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{3}(\lambda,A,A)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,A),A\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)+\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)\big)=0

as required, where in the final step we used (
4.6
).

For
n\geq 3
, we will only give the proof under the vanishing conditions (
5.25
) that we motivated in Section
5.5
; that is, we show
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}F_{A}=\ell_{2}(\lambda,F_{A})
. In this case, all summands of the second line of (
5.30
) vanish except for the term with
j=n
, which is
-\ell_{2}\big(\lambda,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big)
. In the first line, only the summands with
j=0,1
are non-zero. For
j=0
the contributions to the sum over
i
are

\displaystyle\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)=\ell_{n+1}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda),A^{\otimes n}\big)

for each
i=0,1,\dots,n
, where we used
U\mathfrak{v}
-equivariance of the bracket
\ell_{1}
and braided symmetry of the brackets
\ell_{n+1}
on degree
1
elements. For
j=1
the contributions to the sum over
i
can be written in the form

\displaystyle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A),A^{\otimes n-1-i}\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A),\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),A^{\otimes n-1-i}\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{n}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i}),A^{\otimes n-1-i}\big)

for each
i=0,1,\dots,n-1
,
where in the first equality we used braided symmetry of
\ell_{n}
on degree
1
elements, and in the last step we used the first
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity from (
3.2
) when multiplied on the right by
\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes 1\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}
and acted on by the braided antisymmetric map
\ell_{2}
of the first two factors.

Collecting everything together, the condition (
5.30
) in this way reads
