\displaystyle(-1)^{n}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(\ell_{1}(\lambda),A^{\otimes n}\big)=\ell_{2}\big(\lambda,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\ell_{n}\big(\ell_{2}(\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i-1}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\ .

(5.31)

But (
5.31
) is just the braided homotopy relation
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}(\lambda,A^{\otimes n})=0
from (
4.2
) in this case: terms in the sum over
i
in
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}(\lambda,A^{\otimes n})
with
i\neq 1,2,n
all vanish because each one involves a bracket from (
5.25
). After removing the terms involving the vanishing brackets from (
5.25
), the remaining terms in
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}(\lambda,A^{\otimes n})=0
with
i=1,2,n
are precisely the relations (
5.31
) for all
n\geq 3
.

Right gauge covariance

The covariance of (
5.27
) under right gauge transformations is similarly given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}F_{A}=\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\mathsf{R}^{k}(F_{A})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n+1)}}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,(-1)^{i+1}\,\ell_{n+2}\big(A^{\otimes i},F_{A},A^{\otimes n-i},\lambda\big)\ .

The proof is completely analogous to that above and will not be repeated.

Because of this, in the remainder of this paper we will mostly only mention left gauge transformations
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}
explicitly, with the corresponding statements for right gauge transformations being the obvious modifications involving suitable rearrangements of arguments and sign changes. Indeed left and right braided gauge transformations are not independent, as the right transformations are defined in terms of left transformations (cf. (
5.5
)). This will become apparent in Section
5.7
, where we will also see that they both induce the same interdependence among the field equations.

Space of physical states

One of the novelties of braided field theory is the extent to which its symmetries can be regarded as genuine gauge symmetries from a dynamical perspective. Recall that in the classical case of an ordinary field theory on a manifold
M
, by a (infinitesimal) gauge symmetry one usually means an infinite-dimensional symmetry parametrised by sections of a vector bundle over
M
; in the case of Lagrangian field theories these are symmetries of the action functional. As such, they induce redundancies in the degrees of freedom which are solutions of the field equations. For example, suppose that
M
is a globally hyperbolic spacetime. Choosing a Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset M
, along with values of the fields and their jets on
\Sigma
representing initial data, for any solution of the field equations which extends the values of the fields on
M
, one may use the gauge symmetries to produce new solutions with the same initial data. Generally, by Noether’s second theorem, gauge symmetries are in a one-to-one correspondence with differential identities among the field equations, which further exhibits that not all degrees of freedom are independent: the space of
physical
states is the moduli space of classical solutions modulo gauge transformations.

In a braided field theory, the situation is markedly different. In this case there is no properly defined moduli space of classical solutions, as braided gauge transformations do not generally act on the subspace of solutions
A\in V_{1}
to the field equations
F_{A}=0
and one cannot take the quotient by braided gauge transformations. In other words, braided gauge symmetries
do not
produce new solutions of the field equations. To understand this point, let us consider the covariance of the field equations. Let
A\in V_{1}
be a solution of

\displaystyle F_{A}=0\ .

From covariance (
5.28
) it then follows that

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}F_{A}=0

on solutions of
F_{A}=0
, for any gauge parameter
\lambda\in V_{0}
. However, computing the first order variation in the infinitesimal gauge parameter we find

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\,F_{A+t\,\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\ell_{n}(A+t\,\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,\dots,A+t\,\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,\ell_{n}\big(A^{\otimes i},\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-1-i}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m-1)}}{(n+1)!\,m!}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,\ell_{n+1}\big(A^{\otimes i},\ell_{m+1}(\lambda,A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\ .

This differs from the second line of (
5.6
) by the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix factors in the sum over
i
, and we have shown that in general

\displaystyle F_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A}\neq F_{A}+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}F_{A}\ ,

even at order
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{1})
, except in the classical case where
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
and the expected equality holds. This implies that a braided gauge transformation of a solution
A
to the field equations
F_{A}=0
may result in a field with
F_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A}\neq 0
, even at order
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{1})
.

What is then the meaning of a braided gauge symmetry, and why should one even speak of them as “gauge” transformations? The answer comes from a braided version of Noether’s second theorem: Even though the solution generating aspect of gauge transformations is lost, they still induce interdependence of the field equations, and hence gauge redundancies in this sense. These identities follow, analogously to the classical case, from our braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism, and is our next step in the construction of a braided field theory in this formulation.

5.7
Braided Noether identities

The most complicated part of the story, and yet the most important in light of our discussion about the significance of braided gauge symmetries from Section
5.6
, concerns the braided version of the Noether identities. They are relations for the field equations
F_{A}
which hold off-shell, that is, when
F_{A}\neq 0
, and are a consequence of the braided homotopy relations
\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=0
for all
n\geq 1
. In Section
5.8
we will discuss how they correspond to braided gauge symmetries.

Working under the same assumptions (
5.25
), we will prove the general vanishing identity

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}

\displaystyle:=\ell_{1}(F_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell_{2}(F_{A},A)-\ell_{2}(A,F_{A})\big)-\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{2\,n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\Big(\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A)\big)\Big)

(5.32)

\displaystyle=0\ .

We comment on the general case below. Note that, in contrast to the classical case (
2.8
), the operation
{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}
is no longer linear in the field equations
F_{A}
and contains inhomogeneous terms involving brackets of the fields
A
themselves. This is related to the violations of the Bianchi identities which we discusssed at the end of Section
5.4
, and we will come back to this point in our explicit examples later on.

To prove the identity (
5.7
), we first note that, for any
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra with a (braided or strict) cyclic structure of degree
-3
, the second of the conditions in (
5.25
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\ell_{n+1}(F,A_{1},\dots,A_{n})=0\ ,

(5.33)

for all
n\geq 2
,
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in V_{1}
and
F\in V_{2}
. This vanishing condition significantly simplifies the braided homotopy relation
\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=0
from (
4.2
): in this case the terms with
\ell_{n+1-i}
acting then vanish for
i\leq n-2
, and the sum over
i
receives non-zero contributions only from terms with
i=n-1,n
.

The derivation of (
5.7
) follows by considering a particular weighted sum of braided homotopy relations
\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=0
, which vanishes term by term, expanding each
\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})
in brackets, and manipulating the expressions algebraically to make the field equations
F_{A}
appear “maximally”, as many times as possible. We start from

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\,(-1)^{i\,(n-i)}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{i,n-i}}\,\text{sgn}(\sigma)\,\ell_{n+1-i}\circ(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n-i})\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma(A^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{1}(A)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{2}(A,A)\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(A),A\big)+\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{1}(A)\big)\Big)

(5.34)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\bigg((-1)^{n-1}\,\sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{n-1,1}}\,\text{sgn}(\sigma)\,\ell_{2}\circ(\ell_{n-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 284.52756pt+\sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{n,0}}\,\text{sgn}(\sigma)\,\ell_{1}\circ\ell_{n}\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma\bigg)(A^{\otimes n})\ ,

where in the third equality we expanded out the
n=1,2
terms, while using the simplification of
\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})
for
n\geq 3
. Next we notice the last sum over
(n,0)
-shuffled permutations contains only the identity permutation, thus combining the first term, the second term and the
\ell_{1}\circ\ell_{n}
terms of the sum:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}\Big(\ell_{1}(A)-\frac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}(A,A)+\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(A),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{1}(A)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)+n-1}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}_{n-1,1}}\,\text{sgn}(\sigma)\,\ell_{2}\circ(\ell_{n-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma(A^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(F_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(A),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{1}(A)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n-i},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)\ ,

where we cancelled the sign factor
\text{sgn}(\sigma)
with the same sign coming out of the graded (braided) permutation after acting with
{}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\sigma
on
A^{\otimes n}
, since
A
is of degree
1
. Next we separate out the terms with
i=0,n
from the sum over
i
, write
\frac{1}{(n+1)!}=\frac{1}{2\,n!}-\frac{n-1}{2\,(n+1)!}
, and use braided antisymmetry for the
i=n
term to get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(F_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{1}(A),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{1}(A)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\bigg(\frac{1}{2\,n!}-\frac{n-1}{2\,(n+1)!}\bigg)\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\,\Big(\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n}),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\ \sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n-i},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}(F_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell_{2}(F_{A},A)-\ell_{2}(A,F_{A})\big)-\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\frac{n-1}{(n+1)!}\,\Big(\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n}),A\big)-\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n-1)}\ \sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n-i},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)\ ,

(5.35)

where in the second equality we combine the second and third terms with the
\frac{1}{2\,n!}
terms of the first sum.

The expression (
5.7
) is one form of the braided Noether identities. Written in this way, the reduction to the classical case is immediate: when
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
the terms within the sum over
i
are all equal and so the two sums cancel each other by strict antisymmetry of bracket
\ell_{2}
, while the second and third terms likewise combine, thus reducing the identity (
5.7
) to the classical Noether identity (
2.8
) (when (
5.33
) is applied), as expected. The identity (
5.7
) can be written in a slightly simpler fashion by using
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}(A^{\otimes n+1})=0
to rewrite the sum over
i
as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n-i},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)

\displaystyle=\ell_{2}\big(A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big)-\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n}),A\big)-(-1)^{n}\,\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{n+1}(A^{\otimes n+1})\big)\ .

Substituting this in (
5.7
), after a short manipulation of the prefactors we arrive at (
5.7
).

It is clear from this calculation how the braided Noether identities follow for any braided field theory. One simply adds up all the braided homotopy relations
\mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=0
and makes the field equations appear within the other brackets. The field equations
F_{A}
will appear within brackets up to as high order as the brackets which appear in the braided gauge transformation formula (
5.18
), like they did from the restriction (
5.33
) in the present case; many more extra brackets involving solely the fields
A
will appear in the most general case. The details of the general case are beyond the scope of this paper and will be left for future investigations, as the present result is sufficient for all the theories we shall consider.

The correspondence with braided gauge symmetries is immediately apparent in an action formalism for the braided field theory, where the braided Noether identities
{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}
are ‘dual’ to the braided gauge transformations
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A
and
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}A
via a cyclic structure on the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra, as in the classical case from Section
2.2
. Indeed this is how Noether identities are usually derived in classical field theories, and we will discuss this further in Section
5.8
below. But we stress that this is unnnecessary and the Noether identities follow from the known braided homotopy relations in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra approach to field theories.

5.8
Action formulation

The variational formulation of the braided covariant field equations helps to elucidate the meaning of braided gauge symmetries as discussed previously, while also demonstrating some unexpected subtleties and differences in the action principle as compared to the classical case. Although all of the dynamics of a braided field theory can be encoded in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure as we have thus far described, the action formalism will make more clear to what extent particular examples of braided field theories differ from their classical counterparts.

The field equations
F_{A}=0
, given by (
5.27
), can be described as the stationary locus of an action functional
S:V_{1}\to\mathbbm{R}
which is constructed by endowing our
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
with a non-degenerate
strictly
cyclic pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
of degree
-3
, that is

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},v_{1}\rangle=\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{0}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{1})\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{0}\rangle\ ,

(5.36)

and

\displaystyle\langle v_{0},\ell_{n}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\rangle=(-1)^{(|v_{0}|+1)\,n}\ \langle v_{n},\ell_{n}(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1})\rangle\ ,

(5.37)

for all
n\geq 1
and
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
. We can interpret the strict symmetry (
5.36
) as saying that we focus on pairings which are invariant under the natural action of the twisted Hopf algebra. Then the action functional is given exactly as in the classical case.

Definition 5.38
.

The
braided action functional

S:V_{1}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
is

\displaystyle S(A):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\,(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,{n\,(n-1)}}\,\langle A,\ell_{n}(A,\dots,A)\rangle\ ,

(5.39)

on dynamical fields
A\in V_{1}
.

Variational principle

The strictness requirement on the cyclic structure ensures that the critical locus of the action functional (
5.39
) coincides with solutions of the field equations from Section
5.6
. For a general field variation
\delta A
on the tangent space
TV_{1}
to field space, the variation of the action functional (or any other functional of
A\in V_{1}
) is defined in the usual way by

\displaystyle\delta S(A):=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\,S(A+t\,\delta A)\ .

This defines
\delta
as a strict derivation of degree
0
, that is, it obeys an undeformed Leibniz rule with the trivial coproduct
\triangle(\delta)=\delta\otimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta
. We further require that
\delta
commutes with the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrices of the braiding, analogously to the action of the exterior derivative
\mathrm{d}
on twisted differential forms, but in contrast to braided gauge transformations. Similarly to Section
5.2
, we iterate this coproduct to act on maps
\mu_{n}
defined on
\text{\Large$\otimes$}^{n}V
by
\delta\mu_{n}:=\mu_{n}\circ\triangle^{n}(\delta)
; in particular, we define
\delta\langle-,-\rangle:=\langle-,-\rangle\circ\triangle(\delta)
.

Then the variation
\delta S
of the action functional (
5.39
) proceeds identically to the classical case, where both the cyclicity requirement and the derivation properties of
\delta
are the same: we use

\displaystyle\delta\langle A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle=\langle\delta A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\,\langle A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes i},\delta A,A^{\otimes n-i-1})\rangle=(n+1)\,\langle\delta A,\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle\ ,

where in the last step we used strict cyclicity to set all terms in the sum equal to the first term. Using (
5.27
) this shows that

\displaystyle\delta S(A)=\langle\delta A,F_{A}\rangle

(5.40)

for arbitrary variations
\delta A
of the fields. Hence by non-degeneracy of the pairing, the extrema of the action functional (
5.39
) are precisely the solutions
A\in V_{1}
of the field equations
F_{A}=0
.

Braided gauge invariance

The problem of invariance of the action functional (
5.39
) under braided gauge transformations (
5.18
) is much more involved and does not simply follow the classical route. A potentially problematic new feature which immediately arises is the following. Recalling our discussion from Section
5.2
regarding the consistency with braided symmetric maps, the braided Leibniz rule is not generally compatible with the strict symmetry (
5.36
) of the cyclic pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
. Strict symmetry means
\langle-,-\rangle=\langle-,-\rangle\circ\tau_{V,V}^{\sharp}
, but this is not the consistent notion of symmetry for a morphism in the category
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, because it only involves the graded transposition
\tau^{\sharp}
which is
not
the braiding isomorphism in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
unless
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
. As a result
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\langle-,-\rangle\neq\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}(\langle-,-\rangle\circ\tau^{\sharp})
; explicitly

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}v_{1},v_{2}\rangle+\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1}),\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2}\rangle=\langle\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}v_{2},\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1})\rangle+\langle v_{2},\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}v_{1}\rangle\neq\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\langle v_{2},v_{1}\rangle\ ,

(5.41)

which is not generally consistent with
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{2},v_{1}\rangle
unless
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
in which case equality holds.
10
10

10

Strictly speaking, the gauge transformations
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}
are not even defined on maps which do not live in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, so formally this discussion is not even well-defined from the categorical point of view.

Despite this evident inconsistency, the gauge transformation of the action functional (
5.39
)
is
nevertheless well-defined, because the cyclicity condition (
5.37
) ensures compatibility with strict symmetry of the pairing order by order in tensor powers of the fields
A^{\otimes n}
for
n\geq 1
: Using (
5.18
), and repeatedly applying strict symmetry (
5.36
), cyclicity (
5.37
) and the first
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity from (
3.2
), we compute

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\big\langle A,\ell_{n}\big(A^{\otimes n}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}A,\ell_{n}\big(A^{\otimes n}\big)\big\rangle+\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\ell_{n}\big(\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}})(A^{\otimes n})\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle A,\ell_{n}\big(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-1}\big)\big\rangle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}\,\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-i-1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes n-1}),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-1}\big),A\big\rangle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}\,\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-i-2}\big),A\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes n-1})\big),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-1}\big),A\big\rangle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}\,\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i+1}),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,A^{\otimes n-i-2}\big),A\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes n})\big),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\triangle_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}})(A^{\otimes n})\big),A\big\rangle+\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}\big(\ell_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(A^{\otimes n}\big),A\big\rangle\ ,

(5.42)

where in the fourth equality we used

\displaystyle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i-2}\big)=\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i+1}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i-2}\big)

and similarly

\displaystyle\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes n-1})\big),\ell_{m+1}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}\big)\big\rangle=\big\langle\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes n})\big),\ell_{m+1}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}\big)\big\rangle\ ,

for all
m\geq 0
. Hence
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)
is well-defined.

Recall that in the classical case, the exact same cyclicity argument as that used to arrive at (
5.40
) would show that a general gauge transformation of the action functional can be written as
\delta_{\lambda}S(A)=\langle\delta_{\lambda}A,F_{A}\rangle
(cf. (
2.10
)). In this case, a classical gauge transformation can be identified with a specific direction in the tangent space
TV_{1}
to field space, as a special instance of a general variation of the action functional. However, while this identification is essentially tautological in the classical case, it ceases to be true in our braided setting, because general variations and braided gauge transformations are treated differently: a transformation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A
of a field
A\in V_{1}
along a gauge parameter
\lambda\in V_{0}
is no longer a special case of a general variation
\delta A
of the field, because the gauge transformations are now defined as
braided
derivations, in contrast to the general variations. In other words, in the braided case the first equality in (
2.10
) simply no longer holds in general:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)\neq\langle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}A,F_{A}\rangle\ .

(5.43)

This is explicitly evident in the calculation of (
5.8
).

With these preliminary considerations out of the way, we will now establish braided gauge invariance of the action functional:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)=0

(5.44)

for all
\lambda\in V_{0}
and
A\in V_{1}
. Because of (
5.43
), we have to work much harder than in the classical case. By using steps analogous to those in (
5.6
), we compute the gauge variation of the action functional (
5.39
) using (
5.18
) to write

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m-1)}}{(n+2)!\,m!}\,\Big(\big\langle\ell_{m+1}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes m}\big),\ell_{n+1}\big(A^{\otimes n+1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sum_{i=0}^{n}\,\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\big\rangle\Big)\ .

Next we use cyclicity as well as the by now familiar manipulations with the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
3.2
) to isolate the gauge parameter
\lambda
in the left entries of the pairings. This yields

\displaystyle\big\langle\ell_{m+1}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes m}\big),\ell_{n+1}\big(A^{\otimes n+1}\big)\big\rangle=(-1)^{m+1}\,\big\langle\lambda,\ell_{m+1}\big(A^{\otimes m},\ell_{n+1}(A^{\otimes n+1})\big)\big\rangle\ ,

and

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\big\langle A,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i-1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\big\langle A,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A^{\otimes i+1}),\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m}),A^{\otimes n-i-1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\big\langle A,\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\ell_{m+1}(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\lambda),A^{\otimes m})),\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(A^{\otimes i+1}),A^{\otimes n-i-1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\big\langle A,\ell_{n+1}\big(\ell_{m+1}(\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes m})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i+1}),A^{\otimes n-i-1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\big\langle\ell_{m+1}\big(\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes m})\big),\ell_{n+1}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i+1}),A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=(-1)^{m+1}\,\big\langle\lambda,\ell_{m+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes m}),\ell_{n+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i+1}),A^{\otimes n-i})\big)\big\rangle\ ,

for
i=0,1,\dots,n
, where in the first equality we used cyclicity, in the second equality we used the first
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity from (
3.2
), in the third equality we used braided symmetry of the bracket
\ell_{n+1}
, in the fourth equality we used the first
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identity from (
3.2
) again, and in the last two equalities we applied cyclicity again. Thus the gauge transformation is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)+\frac{1}{2}\,m\,(m+1)}}{(n+2)!\,m!}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n+1}\,\big\langle\lambda,\ell_{m+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes m}),\ell_{n+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i}),A^{\otimes n-i+1})\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\,n\,(n+1)}}{n!}\ \sum_{j=0}^{n}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-j+1}\,\frac{(-1)^{j\,(n-j)}}{(n-j+1)\,(n-j+2)}\,\binom{n}{j}

(5.45)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\,\big\langle\lambda,\ell_{j+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes j}),\ell_{n-j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i}),A^{\otimes n-i-j+1})\big)\big\rangle\ ,

for all
\lambda\in V_{0}
.

By collecting terms order by order in tensor powers
A^{\otimes n+1}
, and using non-degeneracy of the cyclic pairing, we see that (
5.44
) is satisfied if

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n-j+1}\,\frac{(-1)^{j\,(n-j)}}{(n-j+1)\,(n-j+2)}\,\binom{n}{j}\,\ell_{j+1}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes j}),\ell_{n-j+1}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A^{\otimes i}),A^{\otimes n-i-j+1})\big)=0

(5.46)

for each
n\geq 0
. This is a consequence of the fact that this expression can be brought to the form of the braided homotopy relations
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}(A^{\otimes n+1})=0
from (
4.2
). The proof follows in exactly the same vein as the proof of braided covariance of the field equations from Section
5.6
, and will not be repeated here; the details are left for the interested reader to fill in.

Braided Noether identities

We can now make a more precise statement about the correspondence between the braided Noether identities discussed in Section
5.7
and braided gauge symmetries of our field theory. Despite the fact that the first equality of (
2.10
) does not carry over to the braided case (cf. (
5.43
)), one can still write the gauge transformation of the action functional in the form

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)=-\langle\lambda,{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}\rangle\ ,

by using cyclicity to isolate the gauge parameter
\lambda
, along with the braided homotopy relations, while making the field equations
F_{A}
appear. In this sense the operation
{\sf d}_{A}
is the ‘braided adjoint’ to
\delta^{\textrm{\tiny L}}_{\lambda}
with respect to the cyclic pairing, and the braided Noether identities
{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}=0
then follow from braided gauge invariance
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}S(A)=0
of the action functional (
5.39
), as discussed above. This is the way that Noether identities of field theories are usually derived in the classical case.

The isolation of
\lambda
was done in the calculation (
5.8
), from which an explicit formula for
{\mathsf{d}}_{A}F_{A}
can be extracted: the weighted sum of braided homotopy relations (
5.7
) is exactly the combination that appears in (
5.8
). What remains to be done is to massage the expression into a form involving
F_{A}
explicitly. However, this is not necessary as we saw in Section
5.7
, as the calculation follows from the braided homotopy relations: the most effective and concise way to derive the braided Noether identities is through the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework which we have developed in this section. Similarly, cyclicity of the pairing implies that right braided gauge invariance
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}S(A)=0
induces the same braided Noether identities
{\sf d}_{A}F_{A}=0
.

Drinfel’d twist deformations of action functionals

In our concrete examples later on, the pairing in a braided field theory used to define an action functional will be obtained by twist deformation quantization of the pairing used in a classical field theory. Note, however, that the resulting action functional from the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation is not generally the same as that obtained by naively twist deforming the Lagrangian of a classical field theory, rather we twist only the structure maps of the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure used for the construction of the classical field theory. We will see explicitly in Section
6.3
that the two prescriptions do not generally define the same noncommutative field theory.

5.9
Example: Braided Chern–Simons theory

In the remainder of this paper we apply the general framework developed in this section to concrete examples of (diffeomorphism-invariant) braided field theories. We start with the simplest illustrative example to close off the present section. Similarly to the classical case, our formalism provides a vast generalization of braided noncommutative Chern–Simons gauge theory on a three-dimensional manifold
M
based on a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, which is the prototypical example of a braided field theory. Starting from the differential graded Lie algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
-valued exterior differential forms on
M
from Section
2.3
, we use Proposition
4.8
to construct the
4
-term differential (graded) braided Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star}\big)
from Section
3.3
with the brackets

\displaystyle\ell_{1}^{\star}(\alpha)=\mathrm{d}\alpha\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)=-[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

for
\alpha,\beta\in V=\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.

Using (
5.18
) we define the (left) braided gauge transformation of a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
by a gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}A:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\lambda)+\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,A)=\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(5.47)

This is just the braided gauge transformation of a connection from Examples
5.6
and
5.24
; in particular, they close the braided Lie algebra (
5.22
) on
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
with bracket
\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
. Using (
5.27
), the field equations
F_{A}^{\star}=0
in
\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
are encoded by

\displaystyle F_{A}^{\star}:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,A)=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(5.48)

which is just the braided curvature of the gauge field
A
from Section
5.4
; in particular, from (
5.28
) we obtain the anticipated braided gauge covariance

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F_{A}^{\star}=\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,F_{A}^{\star})=-[\lambda,F_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

Thus the braided Chern–Simons field equations state that the braided connection
A
is flat, which is just the obvious deformation of the corresponding statement from the classical case.

At this point, however, the classical and braided field theories diverge. Recalling from Section
5.6
that
F^{\star}_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}A}\neq F^{\star}_{A}+\delta_{\lambda}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{A}
, there is no moduli space of flat connections modulo braided gauge transformations, and the space of physical states cannot be described as in the classical case. This deviation is accounted for by the different Noether identity in the braided case, which is no longer simply the Bianchi identity for the braided curvature, which is generally violated as we discussed in Section
5.4
. Using (
5.7
) and the braided homotopy relations, the braided Noether identity in
\Omega^{3}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{A}^{\star}F_{A}^{\star}:=

\displaystyle\,\ell_{1}^{\star}(F_{A}^{\star})-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,F_{A}^{\star})-\ell_{2}^{\star}(F_{A}^{\star},A)\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}F_{A}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,F_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[F_{A}^{\star},A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}F_{A}^{\star}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}F_{A}^{\star}\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ =\ 0\ ,

(5.49)

in agreement with the deformed Bianchi identities (
5.4
).
Thus
{\sf d}_{A}^{\star}
does not coincide with the symmetrized braided covariant derivative
\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\big)
.

Finally, to formulate the braided Chern–Simons action functional, we assume that the three-manifold
M
is compact and oriented, that
\mathfrak{g}
is a quadratic Lie algebra, and that the Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
is Hermitian and compatible with the cyclic structure (
2.11
); in this case the compatibility conditions were discussed in Section
3.3
. By Proposition
4.18
, the pairing defined by

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\star}:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta)\ ,

with
|\alpha|+|\beta|=3
, thus determines a strictly cyclic structure on the underlying braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star}\big)
.

Then we can construct a braided gauge invariant action functional
S_{\star}:\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbbm{R}[[\hbar]]
, for which the braided Chern–Simons field equation
F_{A}^{\star}=0
describes its critical locus, according to the general prescription of (
5.39
):

\displaystyle S_{\star}(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\big\langle A,\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)\big\rangle_{\star}-\frac{1}{6}\,\big\langle A,\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,A)\big\rangle_{\star}=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{3}\,A\wedge_{\star}[A,A]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big)\ .

(5.50)

This is just the expected braided deformation of the classical Chern–Simons action functional (
2.12
) (with the braided Lie bracket).
Using graded cyclicity of the integral over
M
with respect to the twisted exterior
product
\wedge_{\star}
, in the sense of Section
3.3
, along with invariance of the quadratic form
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathfrak{g}
and the usual
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
3.2
), one may check explicitly that this action functional is invariant under the braided gauge transformations (
5.47
), that it gives the field equations defined by (
5.48
) under arbitrary variations of the gauge fields
\delta A
, and that it yields the braided Noether identity (
5.9
) along the lines explained in Section
5.8
using braided gauge invariance of
S_{\star}(A)
and cyclicity of the pairing.

We stress that the constraints imposed in this last step on the manifold
M
, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and the twist
\mathcal{F}
are only needed if one wishes to view the field equations as the stationary locus of an action functional. The braided gauge symmetries and dynamics of the braided field theory can be formulated entirely without these restrictions if one is satisfied with regarding it as a non-Lagrangian field theory, which for many (but not all) considerations is sufficient at the classical level.

6
Noncommutative gravity

6.1
Noncommutative deformations of general relativity

Over the last
20
years there has been an ongoing effort
to construct consistent noncommutative theories of gravity, motivated from several research programmes. On one hand, it is argued that they should serve as low-energy limits of any fully fledged theory of quantum gravity, retaining certain aspects of quantum-induced noncommutativity. On the other hand, T-duality and the conjectural noncommutativity of certain string theoretic backgrounds suggest that gravity ought to be formulated in such terms as well. These noncommutative theories of gravity rely on
the notions of noncommutative spacetime and noncommutative geometry, and in appropriate
limits they reduce to classical general relativity. One of the main problems encountered in these
approaches is the breaking of the diffeomorphism symmetry of general relativity.
In most theories the diffeomorphism symmetry, or at
least a part of it, is broken and one
needs to make sense of this breaking and of the remaining symmetries, if any survive. To put our theories into proper context with previous literature on the subject, we start by briefly reviewing some previous theories of noncommutative gravity based on deformation quantization in the various flavours that are similar in spirit to the approach taken in the present paper: geometric quantization, star-product deformations (equivalently quantization of Poisson structures), and Drinfel’d twist deformations.

In the second order or metric formalism, noncommutative theories of gravity were originally introduced via Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization in the seminal works
[
15
,
12
]
. In these theories one starts from the Hopf algebra of the classical diffeomorphism symmetry of general relativity and applies a Drinfel’d twist to generate the twisted Hopf algebra of diffeomorphisms, exactly as we have done in the present paper. The noncommutative differential geometry is then covariant with respect to the twisted diffeomorphism symmetry
[
8
]
. Consistent noncommutative deformations of the (vacuum) Einstein equations can be written down, and some
particular solutions based on twists constructed from Killing or semi-Killing vector fields have been obtained in
[
47
,
5
]
, as twist deformations of classical solutions and their symmetries.

In the first order or Cartan formalism, noncommutative gravity can be formulated as a
noncommutative gauge theory of the Lorentz or (A)dS group using the enveloping algebra approach and Seiberg–Witten maps
[
38
]
, as discussed in Section
5.3
. A noncommutative deformation of the Einstein–Cartan–Palatini action was discussed in
[
4
,
26
,
24
,
10
]
, and the coupling to matter fields (fermion and gauge fields) was discussed in
[
7
,
35
,
6
,
32
]
; a noncommutative extension of Palatini–Holst theory was considered by
[
28
]
. A common feature of these models is that the first order correction to the classical theory (in the deformation parameter
\hbar
) vanishes if the corresponding action functional is real. The first non-vanishing correction is then at order
\hbar^{2}
. A solution of the field equations corresponding to a deformation of Minkowski spacetime via the Moyal–Weyl twist was found in
[
29
]
. Some of these theories also have a twisted diffeomorphism symmetry realized via the action of the star-Lie derivative from Section
3.4
(see
[
7
,
6
]
). A relation between the Seiberg–Witten map approach and noncommutative theories of gravity via Fedosov deformation quantization of endomorphism bundles was developed in
[
34
]
. Deformed dispersion relations in a
\kappa
-deformation of Minkowski space defined via a Jordanian twist were derived in
[
9
]
.

The main goal of this final section is to apply the formalism of braided field theory that we have developed in Section
5
to construct new braided theories of noncommutative gravity. Our approach has many advantages compared to previous treatments. In particular, our theories are symmetric under braided diffeomorphisms, like the theories of
[
15
,
12
,
7
,
6
]
. However, in contrast to the first order formalism of
[
7
,
6
]
, our approach retains braided versions of all symmetries with good classical limits, without the need of introducing new spurious degrees of freedom that have no interpretation in the classical world (see the discussion in Section
5.3
). Our theories have an action formulation and the coupling to fermion fields is straightforward. In both the metric and Cartan approaches, the failure of twisted diffeomorphisms in generating new classical solutions has always been prevalent (though seemingly not noticed before), and our framework clarifies this unconventional feature (see the discussion in Section
5.6
). We proceed now to our new braided versions of noncommutative gravity, treating separately the three-dimensional and four-dimensional theories in turn.

6.2
Braided gravity in three dimensions

The braided noncommutative deformation of Einstein–Cartan–Palatini gravity in three dimensions follows a similar path to the braided Chern–Simons theory constructed in Section
5.9
. It serves to introduce as well some of the ingredients that will appear later in the more complicated four-dimensional version of the theory. Our first observation is that the differential graded Lie algebra of Section
2.4
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
, that is, the vector space (
2.13
) is a graded
U\mathfrak{v}
-module and the brackets
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
are
U\mathfrak{v}
-equivariant; this stems from the diffeomorphism symmetry of general relativity. We proceed to twist deform it using Proposition
4.8
to a differential (graded) braided Lie algebra, starting from the same graded vector space (
2.13
) and with the same notation. We use the same non-zero brackets
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
, except that now we include an extra term which will accomodate the presence of a cosmological constant
\Lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
; this modifies only the
2
-brackets
\ell_{2}\big((e_{1},\omega_{1})\,,\,(e_{2},\omega_{2})\big)
between fields in (
2.14
) by shifting the first slot with the term
2\,\Lambda\,e_{1}\curlywedge e_{2}

[
30
]
.

Braided
\bm{L_{\infty}}
-algebra

The
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying three-dimensional noncommutative gravity has differential given by

\displaystyle\ell_{1}^{\star}(\xi,\rho)=(0,\mathrm{d}\rho)\ ,\quad\ell_{1}^{\star}(e,\omega)=(\mathrm{d}\omega,\mathrm{d}e)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}^{\star}(E,{\Omega})=(0,\mathrm{d}{\Omega})\ ,

which as always coincides with the classical differential. The
2
-brackets are modified non-trivially from the classical case to

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi_{1},\rho_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\rho_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\big([\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\,,\,-[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star}\rho_{2}-\mathrm{L}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})}^{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\rho_{1})\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(-\rho\star e+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}e\,,\,-[\rho,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\omega\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(-[\rho,E]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}E\,,\,-\rho\star{\Omega}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}{\Omega}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{P})\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}\big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\rho)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(\mathcal{P})\big)+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\mathcal{X}\,,\,-\rho\star\mathcal{P}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\mathcal{P}\Big)\ ,

(6.1)

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e_{1},\omega_{1})\,,\,(e_{2},\omega_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=-\big(\,[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+2\,\Lambda\,e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}e_{2}\,,\,\omega_{1}\wedge_{\star}e_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega_{2})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e_{1})\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}\big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(E)+\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}({\Omega})-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(E)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}({\Omega})\big)\,,\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(E)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)-\omega\wedge_{\star}{\Omega}\Big)\ .

In (
6.1
), the notation
\rho\star e:=\rho\,\triangleright_{\star}e=\rho^{a}{}_{b}\,\star e^{b}\,{\tt E}_{a}
is shorthand for the left braided fundamental representation of
\rho\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{so}(3))
on
e\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3})
, as a special instance of the general definition in (
5.8
); similarly the action of
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{so}(3))
on
e\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{3})
is
\omega\wedge_{\star}e:=\omega\triangleright_{\star}e
, where here the star-exterior product includes matrix multiplication in the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{so}(3)
. For a vector field
\xi\in\Gamma(TM)
, the star-Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}
is defined in Section
3.4
. The twisted product
\curlywedge_{\star}
is the tensor product of the star-exterior product of differential forms on
M
with the undeformed exterior product of multivectors on the vector space
\mathbbm{R}^{3}
; in particular
e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}e_{2}=\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{2})\curlywedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e_{1})
. Note that in the fourth and sixth brackets, for generic twists it is not possible to write the first slots entirely in terms of twisted products
\curlywedge_{\star}
or
\otimes_{\star}
, as the various terms do not commute appropriately; we will come back this point below.

In the following we use some calculational identities which are deformations of those used in
[
30
]
. One has

\displaystyle(\rho\star e)\curlywedge_{\star}E

\displaystyle=-\rho\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(E)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle(\rho\star e)\curlywedge_{\star}e\curlywedge_{\star}e

\displaystyle=-\rho\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(e\curlywedge_{\star}e)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\big)\ .

In the classical case, the second line vanishes, but in the noncommutative theory

\displaystyle(e\curlywedge_{\star}e)\wedge_{\star}e\neq 0

(6.2)

because
1
-forms no longer strictly anticommute with each other.

Braided Lie algebra of gauge symmetries

From (
5.18
), the left braided gauge transformation of a field
A=(e,\omega)
by a gauge parameter
\lambda=(\xi,\rho)
is encoded by

\displaystyle\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}(e,\omega)

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\xi,\rho)+\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)=\big(-\rho\star e+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}e\,,\,\mathrm{d}\rho-[\rho,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\omega\big)\ .

This combines the braided gauge transformations from Examples
5.6
and
5.24
, for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(3)
,
W=\mathbbm{R}^{3}
and
p=1
, with the braided diffeomorphisms from Section
3.4
which abide by the rules of braided gauge transformations spelled out in Section
5.2
.
11
11

11

Analogously to our discussion of left and right braided gauge transformations from Section
5.2
, there are correspondingly a left and a right star-Lie derivative. The star-Lie derivative appearing here acts from the left, but for simplicity we do not indicate this explicitly in the notation
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}
.

According to (
5.22
), these two individual braided Lie algebras of gauge symmetries combine into the single braided Lie algebra with bracket given by

\displaystyle-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi_{1},\rho_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\rho_{2})\big)=\big(-[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\,,\,[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})}^{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\rho_{1})-\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star}\rho_{2}\big)\ .

We identify this combined gauge algebra as the braided semi-direct product

\displaystyle\Gamma(TM)[[\hbar]]\ltimes_{\star}\Omega^{0}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(3)\big)[[\hbar]]

of the braided Lie algebra of vector fields from Section
3.4
, and the braided Lie algebra from Examples
5.6
and
5.24
with
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(3)
,
W=\mathbbm{R}^{3}
and
p=1
. Thus our twisting of both the diffeomorphism and local rotational symmetries in a democratic manner preserves the semi-direct product structure of the classical Lie algebra (
2.15
), and simultaneously avoids the introduction of new degrees of freedom into the theory, in contrast to some previous approaches to noncommutative gravity (cf. Section
5.3
).

Braided curvature and torsion

From (
5.27
), the field equations
F^{\star}_{(e,\omega)}=(0,0)
for the three-dimensional braided ECP theory are encoded through

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{(e,\omega)}=\ell_{1}^{\star}(e,\omega)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)=:(F^{\star}_{e},F^{\star}_{\omega})\ .

The first entry is given by

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{e}=R^{\star}+\Lambda\,e\curlywedge_{\star}e

where

\displaystyle R^{\star}=\mathrm{d}\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\omega,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\omega\wedge_{\star}\omega+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega)\big)

(6.6)

is the braided curvature
2
-form of the spin connection
\omega
, as an example of braided curvature from Section
5.4
. The second entry is given by

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{\omega}=\mathrm{d}e+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\omega\wedge_{\star}e+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\ .

To understand the meaning of this expression, recall from Section
5.4
that there are two separate definitions of braided covariant derivative, and hence there are correspondingly two separate consistent definitions of torsion: A
left
torsion
2
-form

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny L}}:=\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{\omega}e=\mathrm{d}e+\omega\wedge_{\star}e\ ,

(6.7)

and a
right
torsion
2
-form

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny R}}:=\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{\omega}e=\mathrm{d}e+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\ .

(6.8)

Hence the corresponding field equation is given by the symmetrized torsion

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{\omega}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,(T^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny L}}+T^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny R}})

According to (
5.30
), the braided covariance of the field equations is encoded by

\displaystyle\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{(e,\omega)}=\big(\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{e},\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{\omega}\big)=\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(F^{\star}_{e},F^{\star}_{\omega})\big)\ ,

(6.12)

where

\displaystyle\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{e}=-[\rho,F_{e}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}F_{e}^{\star}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{(\xi,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}F^{\star}_{\omega}=-\rho\star F_{\omega}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}F_{\omega}^{\star}\ .

(6.13)

This is in complete harmony with the discussion from Section
5.4
, which shows that the braided curvature is covariant, and that the left and right torsions are both covariant with respect to left braided gauge transformations.

Thus the geometrical meaning of the braided field equations is just a deformation of that from the classical case. The equation
F_{\omega}^{\star}=0
is simply the balanced torsion-free condition
12
12

12

This improves the interpretation of the torsion equation from
[
31
]
, where only the left torsion
2
-form was considered.

T^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny L}}+T^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny R}}=0
, and it would be interesting to see if this can be solved uniquely and algebraically for the spin connection
\omega
in terms of the coframe
e
, as in the classical case where it would yield the Levi–Civita connection. Conditions for retrieving unique connections in similar noncommutative geometry settings have been suggested before (see e.g.
[
3
,
19
]
), and it would be interesting to compare these conditions with our balanced torsion-free condition. The equation
F_{e}^{\star}=0
is the braided analog of the classical Einstein equation with cosmological constant in our formalism (before inserting the Levi–Civita connection). We stress that our field equations are different from those previously suggested for the first order formulation of noncommutative gravity
[
4
]
, already at the kinematic level: in our approach the internal degrees of freedom are braided and hence there are no new dynamical fields introduced from having to extend the underlying Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(3)
.

Our remarks from Section
5.6
concerning the space of classical solutions of course also apply here. In particular, a braided diffeomorphism
\delta^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}_{(\xi,0)}(e,\omega)
can map a classical solution into a field configuration which no longer solves the field equations; for example, for
\Lambda=0
, a braided flat noncommutative spacetime can be braided diffeomorphic to a braided curved noncommutative spacetime. This peculiar feature appears not only in our formalism, but also in the many earlier treatments of noncommutative gravity which used an underlying symmetry principle based on twisted diffeomorphisms (cf. Section
6.1
), which was discussed in Section
3.4
. As far as we are aware, this salient point has not been mentioned or appreciated before. According to the general framework of braided field theory that we have developed in the present paper, the meaning of the local symmetries in noncommutative gravity are captured by braided Noether identities.

Braided Noether identities

By (
5.7
), the gauge redundancies of braided gravity in three dimensions are encoded by the braided Noether identities

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{(e,\omega)}^{\star}F^{\star}_{(e,\omega)}

\displaystyle=\ell_{1}^{\star}\big(F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star})\big)-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star})\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)\Big)

(6.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\frac{1}{4}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(e,\omega)\,,\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(e,\omega),(e,\omega))\big)\ =\ (0,0)\ ,

where
\mathsf{R}_{k}(e,\omega):=\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(e),\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\big)
, and we used the slightly simpler form (
5.7
).
They lead to a pair of differential identities among the field equations (
6.2
) and (
6.2
).

The Noether identity corresponding to braided local
\mathfrak{so}(3)
rotations is given by the second slot of (
6.14
). Compared to the classical case
[
30
]
, it now acquires a contribution from the cosmological constant due to the braiding, in addition to the usual inhomogeneous terms:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{\omega}F^{\star}_{\omega}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{\omega}

\displaystyle F_{\omega}^{\star}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(F^{\star}_{e})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)-F^{\star}_{e}\wedge_{\star}e\big)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big(-[\omega,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}e+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega\wedge_{\star}\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-2\,\Lambda\,(e\curlywedge_{\star}e)\wedge_{\star}e-2\,\Lambda\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(e\curlywedge_{\star}e)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\big)\ =\ 0\ .

(6.15)

The last two terms here vanish in the classical case, but they are generally non-vanishing in the noncommutative case due to (
6.2
). This identity can also be derived directly from the braided deformations of the first Bianchi identity:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{\omega}T_{\textrm{\tiny L}}^{\star}

\displaystyle=R^{\star}\wedge_{\star}e+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\omega\wedge_{\star}\omega\wedge_{\star}e-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}e\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{\omega}T_{\textrm{\tiny R}}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{k}(R^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{m}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{m}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\ ,

which follow easily by taking the exterior derivatives of (
6.7
) and (
6.8
), respectively.
In the classical case, the last two terms on the right-hand sides of both equations cancel and we recover the expected identity
\mathrm{d}^{\omega}T=R\wedge e
, which is equivalent to (
6.2
) in that case.

The Noether identity corresponding to braided diffeomorphisms is given by the first slot of (
6.14
). Given the form of the classical map
\ell_{2}:V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\to V_{3}
from (
2.14
), we have to use the defining expression
\ell^{\star}_{2}\big((e,\omega),(\mathcal{F}_{e},\mathcal{F}_{\omega})\big):=\ell_{2}\big(\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e,\omega),\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(\mathcal{F}_{e},\mathcal{F}_{\omega})\big)
directly in terms of the undeformed classical bracket. Expanding (
6.14
) using classical antisymmetry of brackets in the third line, we find

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}\big(N^{\star}_{\mu}(F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star},e,\omega)\big)\ =\ 0\ ,

(6.16)

where the
3
-form
N^{\star}_{\mu}(F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star},e,\omega)\in\Omega^{3}(M,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{3}\mathbbm{R}^{3})[[\hbar]]
is given by

\displaystyle N^{\star}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(F^{\star}_{e})+\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(F^{\star}_{\omega})-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(F^{\star}_{e})-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(F^{\star}_{\omega})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(F^{\star}_{e})+\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(F^{\star}_{\omega})-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(e)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(F^{\star}_{e})-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(F^{\star}_{\omega})\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,-\frac{1}{4}\,\Big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\big([\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega),\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+2\,\Lambda\,\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\curlywedge_{\star}e\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\big(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}e-\mathsf{R}_{m}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{m}\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(e)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\big([\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega),\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}+2\,\Lambda\,\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\curlywedge_{\star}e\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\big(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}e-\mathsf{R}_{m}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{m}\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\big)\Big)\ .

This expression does not immediately involve the star-contraction defined by
\iota_{\xi}^{\star}\alpha=\iota_{\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\xi)}\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(\alpha)
for
\xi\in\Gamma(TM)
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
. Nevertheless, the expression (
6.16
) is globally well-defined on
M
as an element of
\Omega^{1}(M)\otimes\Omega^{3}(M)[[\hbar]]
. In some special cases where the legs of the twist
\mathcal{F}
commute with the local basis vector fields, we may write the braided diffeomorphism Noether identity in a slightly more compact form; for example, this holds for abelian twists locally around every point of
M
outside a set of measure zero
[
5
,
49
]
.

Example 6.17
.

Consider the Moyal–Weyl twist
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}={\sf f}^{k}_{\theta}\otimes{\sf f}_{\theta k}
on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{3}
from Examples
3.5
and
3.9
. Then
\iota_{\mu}\circ\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}^{k}=\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}^{k}\circ\iota_{\mu}
since
\iota_{\mu}\circ\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\nu}}=\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\nu}}\circ\iota_{\mu}+\iota_{[\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{v}}}=\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\nu}}\circ\iota_{\mu}
by the Cartan structure equations, and
\iota_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}=\iota_{\mu}
since
\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\nu}}\partial_{\mu}=[\partial_{\nu},\partial_{\mu}]_{\mathfrak{v}}=0
in a holonomic basis. In this case we have

\displaystyle\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta k}(F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e})=\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e}

and

\displaystyle\iota_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta k}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}^{k}(F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e})=\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta}^{k}(F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e})\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{\theta k}(\iota_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}e)=F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e}\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e\ ,

with analogous expressions for the rest of the terms. Since also
\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\nu}}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}=0
, it follows that the expression for the braided diffeomorphism Noether identity (
6.16
) simplifies slightly to an expression in
\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{3})\otimes_{\star_{\theta}}\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{3})[[\hbar]]
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes_{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{Tr}\big(N_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}(F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star},e,\omega)\big)=0

(6.18)

with

\displaystyle N_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e}+\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\omega\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{\omega}-\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e}-\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\omega\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{\omega}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e}\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}e+F^{\star_{\theta}}_{\omega}\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\omega-\mathrm{d}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{e}\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}e-\mathrm{d}F^{\star_{\theta}}_{\omega}\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\omega\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,-\frac{1}{4}\,\Big(\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}_{l}(e)\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}[\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega),\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star_{\theta}}+2\,\Lambda\,e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}e

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega)\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\big(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega)\wedge_{\star_{\theta}}e-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star_{\theta}}\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathsf{R}_{l}(e)\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}[\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega),\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star_{\theta}}+2\,\Lambda\,e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\iota_{\mu}^{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}e\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}e

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt-\iota^{\star_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega)\curlywedge_{\star_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}\big(\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega)\wedge_{\star_{\theta}}e-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star_{\theta}}\mathsf{R}^{k}\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\big)\Big)\ .

Action functional

The variational principle for three-dimensional braided gravity follows, similarly to the braided Chern–Simons theory of Section
5.9
, from choosing a Hermitian Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
which is compatible with the cyclic structure of the classical ECP theory defined by (
2.16
) and (
2.17
). Using Proposition
4.18
, we can then turn
(V[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star})
into a strictly cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra with cyclic pairing defined by

\displaystyle\langle(e,\omega)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\rangle_{\star}:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(e\curlywedge_{\star}E+{\Omega}\curlywedge_{\star}\omega\big)\ ,

(6.19)

on
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
and

\displaystyle\langle(\xi,\rho)\,,\,({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{P}})\rangle_{\star}:=\int_{M}\,\iota_{\xi}^{\star}{\mathcal{X}}+\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\rho\curlywedge_{\star}{\mathcal{P}}\big)\ ,

(6.20)

on
V_{0}\otimes V_{3}
.

According to (
5.39
), the action functional is then given by

\displaystyle S_{\star}(e,\omega)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big\langle(e,\omega)\,,\,\ell_{1}^{\star}(e,\omega)\big\rangle_{\star}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big\langle(e,\omega)\,,\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)\big\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\Big(e\curlywedge_{\star}R^{\star}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\,e\curlywedge_{\star}e\curlywedge_{\star}e\Big)=\int_{M}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,\Big(e^{a}\wedge_{\star}R^{\star bc}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\,e^{a}\wedge_{\star}e^{b}\wedge_{\star}e^{c}\Big)\ .

In the second line we used invariance of a top-vector under the left and right braided actions by
\mathfrak{so}(3)
-valued
1
-forms, analogously to the classical case
[
30
]
order by order in the formal power series expansion of the twist in
\hbar
; for example

\displaystyle 0=(\omega\curlywedge_{\star}e)\,_{\star\!\!}\triangleleft\omega=-\omega\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)\big)+[\mathsf{R}_{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega)]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}\curlywedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{l}(e)\ ,

and so on. This is just the expected deformation of the classical action functional (
2.4
); it is the unique action functional constructed from the braided pairing, and the corresponding polynomial orders in the coframe field and the curvature, with the correct classical form when
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
.

Using the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
3.2
), the cyclicity of integration (
3.10
) with respect to the star-product
\wedge_{\star}
, and the invariance of a top-vector under braided
\mathfrak{so}(3)
rotations, one can directly check that varying this action functional with respect to
e
gives the field equation
F_{e}^{\star}=0
defined in (
6.2
), while varying with respect to
\omega
gives the field equation
F_{\omega}^{\star}=0
defined in (
6.2
), as was guaranteed by the general theory of Section
5.8
. Also in agreement with the general theory, one checks invariance of the action functional
\delta_{(0,\rho)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}S_{\star}(e,\omega)=0
under braided
\mathfrak{so}(3)
rotations using braided covariance
\delta_{\rho}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}R^{\star}=-[\rho,R^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}^{\star}
and
\mathfrak{so}(3)
top-vector invariance such as

\displaystyle\rho\star(e\curlywedge_{\star}R^{\star})=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\rho)\cdot\bar{\sf f}_{k}(e\curlywedge_{\star}R^{\star})=0\ ,

as well as invariance
\delta_{(\xi,0)}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}S_{\star}(e,\omega)=0
under braided diffeomorphisms using the classical Cartan formula for the Lie derivative and Stokes’ theorem order by order in
\hbar
. These two braided gauge invariances further imply respectively the braided Noether identities (
6.2
) along the lines discussed in Section
5.8
, though again the most effective and concise way to do this is through the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework of this section.

6.3
Braided gravity in four dimensions

Both the braided Chern–Simons theory from Section
5.9
and the three-dimensional braided ECP theory from Section
6.2
are based on differential (graded) braided Lie algebras, while their field equations and variational principle could have been more or less guessed from the outset by simply replacing ordinary products of fields with star-products. This does not preclude, however, the different kinematical sector of these theories due to the braided Lie brackets, as well as the novel general nature of classical solutions in braided field theory and the corresponding modified forms of gauge redundancies through the braided Noether identities. We will now discuss a highly non-trivial example which is based on an
L_{\infty}
-algebra which is not simply a differential graded Lie algebra. It does not follow the naive deformations of the three-dimensional theories studied thus far, and it illustrates the novelty of our approach in generating new noncommutative field theories with interesting symmetry structures. Indeed, we shall now derive a new noncommutative theory of gravity in four dimensions by applying the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework to the classical four-dimensional ECP theory.

Braided
\bm{L_{\infty}}
-algebra

Let
M
be an oriented parallelizable four-manifold. The
4
-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying the classical Einstein–Cartan–Palatini formulation of general relativity on
M
with cosmological constant
\Lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
was developed in detail by
[
30
]
for an arbitrary signature pseudo-Riemannian structure on
M
; for definiteness, we focus on the case of Euclidean signature, but everything we do holds identically for arbitrary signature
(p,q)
(with
p+q=4
): we simply replace the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(4)
and its fundamental representation
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
by their indefinite versions
\mathfrak{so}(p,q)
and
\mathbbm{R}^{p,q}
. As in the three-dimensional case, this defines an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
\mathscr{M}^{\sharp}
of
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded
U\mathfrak{v}
-modules. Then the underlying graded vector space is given by

\displaystyle V:=V_{0}\oplus V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\oplus V_{3}

where

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(TM)\times\Omega^{0}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(4)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{1}(M,\mathbbm{R}^{4})\times\Omega^{1}\big(M,\mathfrak{so}(4)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{3}\big(M,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{3}\mathbbm{R}^{4}\big)\times\Omega^{3}\big(M,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathbbm{R}^{4}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{1}\big(M,\Omega^{4}(M)\big)\times\Omega^{4}\big(M,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathbbm{R}^{4}\big)\ .

We use the same conventions and notation for fields as we did in the case of three-dimensional gravity from Sections
2.4
and
6.2
.
Following the prescription of Section
4.2
, we twist deform the classical brackets written down in
[
30
, Section 8.2]
, which leads to a
4
-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star},\ell_{3}^{\star})
with the following non-vanishing braided brackets.

The differential
\ell_{1}^{\star}
is given by

\displaystyle\ell_{1}^{\star}(\xi,\rho)=(0,\mathrm{d}\rho)\ ,\quad\ell_{1}^{\star}(e,\omega)=(0,0)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}^{\star}(E,{\Omega})=(0,-\mathrm{d}{\Omega})\ .

(6.21)

The
2
-bracket
\ell_{2}^{\star}
is defined by

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi_{1},\rho_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\rho_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\big([\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\,,\,-[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star}\rho_{2}-\mathrm{L}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})}^{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\rho_{1})\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell^{\star}_{2}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(-\rho\star e+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}e\,,\,-[\rho,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\omega\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(-\rho\star E+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}E\,,\,-[\rho,{\Omega}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}{\Omega}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\rho)\,,\,({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{P}})\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}\big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\rho)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}({\mathcal{P}})\big)+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}{\mathcal{X}}\,,\,-[\rho,{\mathcal{P}}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}{\mathcal{P}}\Big)\ ,

(6.22)

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e_{1},\omega_{1})\,,\,(e_{2},\omega_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=-\big(e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{2})\curlywedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega_{1})\,,\,e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}e_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{2})\curlywedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e_{1})\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(E,{\Omega})\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}\big(\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(E)-\iota_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}({\Omega})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(e)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}(E)+\iota_{\mu}\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}(\omega)\curlywedge\mathrm{d}\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}({\Omega})\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\tfrac{3}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(E)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e)+[\omega,{\Omega}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}\Big)\ ,

with all other
2
-brackets equal to zero. Finally, there is a single non-zero
3
-bracket
\ell_{3}^{\star}
defined on fields by

\displaystyle\ell_{3}^{\star}\big((e_{1},\omega_{1})\,,\,(e_{2},\omega_{2})\,,\,(e_{3},\omega_{3})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\Big(e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}[\omega_{2},\omega_{3}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{2})\curlywedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega_{1}),\omega_{3}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{3})\curlywedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star})

\displaystyle\hskip 341.43306pt+6\,\Lambda\,e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}e_{2}\curlywedge_{\star}e_{3}\ ,

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pte_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}(\omega_{2}\wedge_{\star}e_{3})+e_{1}\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\omega_{3})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e_{2})\big)

(6.23)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{2})\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega_{1})\wedge_{\star}e_{3}\big)+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{2})\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\omega_{3})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{l}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e_{3})\curlywedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega_{1}\wedge_{\star}e_{2})+\mathsf{R}_{k}\mathsf{R}_{l}(e_{3})\curlywedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{m}\mathsf{R}^{l}(\omega_{2})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{m}\mathsf{R}^{k}(e_{1})\big)\Big)\ .

Note that the differential of a generic field
A=(e,\omega)\in V_{1}
vanishes, which will lead to some simplifications in the calculations involving the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra brackets below.

The derivation of the braided gauge symmetries of noncommutative gravity in four dimensions is identical to that in three dimensions which was presented in Section
6.2
, and so will not be repeated here; the only change is that the Lie algebra of rotations
\mathfrak{so}(3)
is replaced with its counterpart
\mathfrak{so}(4)
appropriate to four dimensions. We shall therefore dive straight away into a description of the novel dynamics of the braided gravity theory, where we will uncover many unexpected and surprising features.

Field equations

By (
5.27
), the field equations
F_{(e,\omega)}^{\star}=(0,0)
of the braided ECP theory in four dimensions are given by

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{(e,\omega)}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ell_{3}^{\star}\big((e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\,,\,(e,\omega)\big)=:(F_{e}^{\star},F_{\omega}^{\star})\ .

Expanding the brackets, the components are given by

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{e}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(e\curlywedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\omega+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e)\curlywedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega)\big)+\Lambda\,e\curlywedge_{\star}e\curlywedge_{\star}e

\displaystyle\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big(e\curlywedge_{\star}[\omega,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e)\curlywedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\omega),\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(e)\curlywedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([\omega,\omega]_{\mathfrak{so}(4)}^{\star})\big)\ ,

(6.24)

and

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{\omega}
